vanishes almost everywhere it follows that

\mathcal{T}_{n}(\varphi dy^{1}\wedge dy^{2})=0
for all
\varphi
as in
(
4.30
). Then for
2
-forms
\omega=\varphi dy^{1}\wedge dy^{2}

the convergence

T_{k}(\omega)\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{n}(\omega)
is achieved.
We are then left to prove that for all
2
-forms
\omega
with
\omega(x,y)=\varphi(x,y)dx^{\alpha}\wedge dy^{\beta}
,
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
|\alpha|+|\beta|=2
, and
|\beta|\leq 1
, it holds

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))M^{\beta}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\nabla u_{k}(x))~dx=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x,u(x))M^{\beta}_{\bar{\alpha}}(\nabla u)(1-\zeta_{n}(x))~dx.

(4.31)

To simplify the argument we treat separately the cases
\omega=\varphi(x,y)dx^{1}\wedge dx^{2}
and
\omega=\varphi(x,y)dx^{i}\wedge dy^{j}
for some
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. In the former case we simply have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))~dx=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))\chi_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}(x)~dx.

Then, using that
u_{k}\rightarrow u
uniformly in
\Omega\setminus D_{k}

(see (
4.7
), Lemma

4.2
(ii) and (
4.27
))
and

\chi_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\to\chi_{\Omega}-\zeta_{n}

{\rm weakly}^{*}
in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, it follows

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))~dx=\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x,u(x))(1-\zeta_{n}(x))~dx=\mathcal{T}_{n}(\omega).

Assume now
\omega=\varphi(x,y)dx^{i}\wedge dy^{j}
,
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,
i\neq j
.
In this case (
4.31
) reads as

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\Big(\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))D_{\bar{i}}[(u_{k}(x))_{j}]~dx-\int_{\Omega}\varphi(x,u(x))D_{\bar{i}}u_{j}(x)(1-\zeta_{n}(x))~dx\Big)=0,

with
\bar{i}=\{1,2\}\setminus\{i\}
.
Since

\chi_{D_{k}}\to\zeta_{n}

{\rm weakly}^{*}
in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
,
this is equivalent to proving

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\Big(\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))D_{\bar{i}}[(u_{k}(x))_{j}]~dx-\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u(x))D_{\bar{i}}u_{j}(x)~dx\Big)=0.

The quantity between parentheses on the left-hand side can be written as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\Big(\varphi(x,u_{k}(x))-\varphi(x,u(x))\Big)D_{\bar{i}}[(u_{k}(x))_{j}]~dx+\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u(x))\Big(D_{\bar{i}}[(u_{k}(x))_{j}]-D_{\bar{i}}u_{j}(x)\Big)~dx,

and we see that the first integral tends to zero
as
k\to+\infty
, since
u_{k}\rightarrow u
uniformly in

\Omega\setminus D_{k}
,
\varphi
is Lipschitz continuous,
and the
L^{1}(\Omega)
-norm
of

D_{\bar{i}}[(u_{k})_{j}]
is uniformly bounded with respect to

k
.
The second integral can be instead
integrated by parts
13
13

13

From Lemma
4.2
(i),

D_{k}
has rectifiable boundary;
moreover,
\varphi(\cdot,u_{k}(\cdot))
is Lipschitz.
We can then apply a version of the Gauss-Green
theorem, see for instance

[
24
, pag. 124, exercise 12.12]
.

,
obtaining

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\varphi(x,u(x))(D_{\bar{i}}[(u_{k}(x))_{j}]-D_{\bar{i}}u_{j}(x))~dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{k}}\varphi(x,u(x))((u_{k}(x))_{j}-u_{j}(x))\nu_{\bar{i}}(x)~d\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)-\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}D_{\bar{i}}(\varphi(x,u(x)))((u_{k}(x))_{j}-u_{j}(x))~dx

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle~\textrm{I}_{k}+\textrm{II}_{k}.

Thanks to the fact that
\varphi
is bounded
and that
|(u_{k})_{j}(x)-u_{j}(x)|\leq d_{k}(x)=\lambda_{k}
on
\partial D_{k}
, we
conclude by Corollary
4.3
that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}{\rm I}_{k}=0
.
Moreover

\displaystyle{\rm II}_{k}=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\partial_{x_{\bar{i}}}\varphi(x,u(x))((u_{k}(x))_{j}-u_{j}(x))dx

\displaystyle-\sum_{l=1}^{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\partial_{y_{l}}\varphi(x,u(x))D_{\bar{i}}u_{l}(x)((u_{k})_{j}(x)-u_{j}(x))dx=:{\rm II}_{k,1}+{\rm II}_{k,2}.

Then

\lim_{k\to+\infty}{\rm II}_{k,1}=\lim_{k\to+\infty}{\rm II}_{k,2}=0
,
since
the partial derivatives of
\varphi
are bounded,

D_{\bar{i}}u\in L^{1}(\Omega\setminus D_{k},\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

|(u_{k})_{j}-u_{j}|\leq d_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}
on

\Omega\setminus D_{k}
, and
\lim_{k\to+\infty}\lambda_{k}=0
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The mass of the current
T_{k}
is given by

\displaystyle|T_{k}|=\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}}dx.

(4.32)

To see (
4.32
) we choose a
2
-form

\omega\in\mathcal{D}^{2}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
as

\omega:=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=2}\varphi_{\bar{\alpha}\beta}dx^{\alpha}\wedge dy^{\beta},\qquad\|\omega\|\leq 1,

set
14
14

14

Here
\alpha
and
\beta
run over all the multi-indeces in
\{1,2\}
with the constraint
|\alpha|+|\beta|=2
.

\widehat{\omega}(x,y)=:(\varphi_{\bar{\alpha}\beta}(x,y))\in\mathbb{R}^{6},
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k}(x)):=(1,D_{1}[(u_{k}(x))_{1}],D_{2}[(u_{k}(x))_{1}],D_{1}[(u_{k}(x))_{2}],D_{2}[(u_{k}(x))_{2}],0)\in\mathbb{R}^{6}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathbb{R},

so that

\displaystyle T_{k}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\langle\widehat{\omega}(x,u_{k}(x)),\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k}(x))\rangle dx

(4.33)

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\omega}\|\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k}(x))|dx\leq\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}}dx.

To prove the converse inequality,
choosing
\widehat{\omega}(x,y)=\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k}(x))}{|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k}(x))|}

would give the equality in (
4.33
).
However,

\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k})}{|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k})|}
is not necessarily of class
C^{\infty}_{c}
, so
we need to use the density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
in

L^{1}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2})

(here we use that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u_{k})}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{6})
since
u_{k}
is Lipschitz continuous).

With a similar argument, setting

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u(x)):=(1-\zeta_{n}(x)){\mathcal{M}}(\nabla u(x))\in\mathbb{R}^{6},\quad x\in\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}

we can show that the total mass of

\mathcal{T}_{n}
in
(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given by

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{n}|_{(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}=\int_{\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)||1-\zeta_{n}|~dx.

(4.34)

4.2
Estimate of the mass of

\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
over
\Omega\setminus D_{k}

We denote by
\Phi_{k}=\Phi_{u_{k}}={\rm Id}\bowtie u_{k}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
the map

\displaystyle\Phi_{k}(x)=(x,u_{k}(x)),

(4.35)

in such a way that
\Phi_{k}(\Omega)=G_{u_{k}}
, with

G_{u_{k}}=\{(x,y)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}:y=u_{k}(x)\}

the graph of
u_{k}
.

We denote as usual by

\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2})

(4.36)

the integral current
supported by the graph of
u_{k}
.

We now want to estimate the area of the graph of
u_{k}
over
the set
(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\setminus D_{k}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,l)
satisfy (
4.4
) and (
4.5
),

n\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\lambda_{k})
be as in Lemma

4.2
, and let
D_{k}
be as in
(
4.27
). Then

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx\geq\int_{\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx-\frac{1}{n}-\frac{2}{\varepsilon n}.

(4.37)

Proof.

Set

\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}
.
Since, by definition,
T_{k}
vanishes on smooth
2
-forms
supported in
(D_{k}\cap\Omega_{\varepsilon})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
we employ
(
4.32
)
to obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}}~dx\geq\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}|T_{k}|_{(\Omega_{\varepsilon}{\setminus D_{k}})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}

(4.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}|T_{k}|_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geq|\mathcal{T}_{n}|_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}},

where we use that
(T_{k})
weakly converges to
\mathcal{T}_{n}

(Lemma
4.5
), and the weak lower semicontinuity of the mass.
In turn, from (
4.34
) and (
4.29
),

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{n}|_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)||1-\zeta_{n}|~dx\geq\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx-\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)||\zeta_{n}|~dx

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx-\|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\|\zeta_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx-\frac{1}{n}\|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

Next, using

\sqrt{1+z^{2}}\leq 1+|z|
and
15
15

15

D_{i}u_{j}(x)=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{|x|}-\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{|x|^{3}}
,
hence
\sum_{ij}(D_{i}u_{j}(x))^{2}=\frac{2}{|x|^{2}}+2\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}}{|x|^{6}}\leq\frac{4}{|x|^{2}}
.

|\nabla u(x)|\leq\frac{2}{|x|}

which, on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
,
is bounded by
2/\varepsilon
, we also get

\|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}=\|\sqrt{1+|\nabla u|^{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leq 1+\frac{2}{\varepsilon}.

We deduce

|\mathcal{T}_{n}|_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geq\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx-\frac{1}{n}-\frac{2}{\varepsilon n}.

From
(
4.2
) and
(
4.38
)
inequality (
4.37
) follows.
∎

5
The maps
\Psi_{k}
,
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}
, and the currents
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
,

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
,
\mathcal{E}_{k}

Recalling that

D_{k}
is
defined in (
4.27
) and (
4.9
),
in Section

4.2

we have estimated the area of the graph of
u_{k}
over

\Omega\setminus D_{k}
. The next step, which is considerably more
difficult,
is to estimate this area over
D_{k}
,
and this will be splitted in several parts (Sections

6
-
9
).
After introducing some preliminaries in
Section
5.1
,
the first step is to reduce the graph of
u_{k}

(a surface of codimension
2
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
)
to a suitable rectifiable set (
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
and their projections)
of codimension
1
sitting
in
\overline{C}_{l}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
In this section we introduce all various objects needed to prove
the lower bound.

Definition 5.1

(
The map
\Psi_{k}
)
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
we define the map

\Psi_{k}=\Psi_{u_{k}}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}=\mathbb{R}_{|x|}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{{\rm target}}

as

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}(x):=

\displaystyle(|x|,u_{k}(x))\qquad\forall x\in\Omega.

(5.1)

Notice that

\Psi_{k}
takes values in

\overline{C_{l}}
, and is Lipschitz continuous.
Moreover
\Psi_{k}=R\circ\Phi_{k}
,
where
\Phi_{k}={\rm Id}\bowtie u_{k}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{4}
is defined
in (
4.35
),
and
R:\mathbb{R}^{4}\ni(x,y)\mapsto(|x|,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
By the area formula and since
{\rm Lip}(R)=1

we have

\displaystyle\int_{B}(\nabla\Psi_{k}^{T}\nabla\Psi_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}~dx\leq\int_{B}(\nabla\Phi_{k}^{T}\nabla\Phi_{k})^{\frac{1}{2}}~dx=\int_{B}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx=|\mathcal{G}_{u_{k}}|_{B\times\mathbb{R}^{2}},

for any Borel set
B\subseteq\Omega
.

5.1
The sets
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
and the currents

(\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

We start noticing that

\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus D_{k})\subset\overline{C}_{l}\setminus C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N},

(5.2)

where we recall that

C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
is defined in (
2.4
).
Indeed, since
\Omega\setminus D_{k}\subseteq\{d_{k}\leq\lambda_{k}\}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\lambda_{k}\geq|u_{k}(x)-\frac{x}{|x|}|\geq\textrm{dist}(u_{k}(x),\mathbb{S}^{1})=1-|u_{k}(x)|,\qquad x\in\Omega\setminus D_{k},

(5.3)

so that
|u_{k}(x)|\geq 1-\lambda_{k}
.
In particular

\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})\subset\overline{C}_{l}\setminus C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}),\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.4)

As a consequence, since the map
\Psi_{k}
is Lipschitz continuous, we have:

Corollary 5.2
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}

the integral
2
-current

(\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

is boundaryless in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
.

Observe that
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
is rectifiable
and contains
16
16

16

It could be
different because of
possible cancellations.

the support of
(\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
;
also
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
is contained in

[0,l)\times\overline{B}_{1}
. Specifically, the fact that
C_{l}
has axial
coordinate in
(-1,l)
and not in
(0,l)

will be convenient in order to control the behaviour of
(\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
on
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Definition 5.3

(
The projection
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}
)
.

We let

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}=\pi_{\lambda_{k}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})

(5.5)

be the
orthogonal projection onto the compact convex set

\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
.

In Section
5.2
we project

\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
on
\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
in order to get a
rectifiable set (and its associated current)
whose area (counted with multiplicity) is less than
or equal to the area of the original set; the area
of the projected set, in turn, gives a lower bound for
the mass of
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
over
D_{k}

(see formulas (
5.7
) and (
5.11
)).
Then, as a second step,
we symmetrize
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
using the cylindrical
rearrangement introduced in Section
3
to get a still smaller (in area) object. The
estimate of the area of the symmetrized object is divided in two parts:
the first one (Section
7
)
deals with

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}))
whose symmetrized
set can be seen as the generalized graph of a
suitable polar function.
In Section
8

we deal with the second part,
where we estimate the area of the symmetrization obtained from
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\cap{\rm B}_{\varepsilon})
.
In Sections
9
and
11
,
we collect our estimates
and we utilize the symmetrized object
as a competitor for a suitable non-parametric Plateau problem. To do this we
need to glue to the obtained rectifiable set some artificial surfaces,
whose areas are controlled and are infinitesimal
in the limit as
k\rightarrow+\infty
. This limit is taken
only at the end of Section
11
, allowing us to analyse a non-parametric Plateau problem
whose boundary condition does not depend on
k
, so that also its solution
does not depend on
k
.
The area of such a solution will be the lower bound for the area of
the rectifiable set

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))\cup\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\cap{\rm B}_{\varepsilon})
,
and then finally for the area of the graph of
u_{k}
on
D_{k}
.

5.2
Construction of the current
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}

via the currents
\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
\mathcal{W}_{k}

We are interested in the part of the set
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
included
in
\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
;
we need an explicit description of the boundary of
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
,
and to this aim
we compose
\Psi_{k}
with the projection
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}
in (
5.5
).

Definition 5.4

(
Projection of
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
:
the current

{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
)
.

We define
the current
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}{\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(C_{l})}

as

{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}:=(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

(5.6)

Remark 5.5
.

In general

\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\subseteq\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})\cup(\overline{C_{l}}\setminus C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}))
,
while
{\rm spt}({\mathfrak{D}}_{k})\subseteq\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})
.

Since
{\rm Lip}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}})=1
,
the map
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}
does not increase the area, and
therefore

\int_{D_{k}}|J(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})|~dx\leq\int_{D_{k}}|J(\Psi_{k})|~dx\leq|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{D_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}},

(5.7)

\int_{D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})}|J(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})|~dx\leq\int_{D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})}|J(\Psi_{k})|~dx\leq|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{(D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))\times\mathbb{R}^{2}},

(5.8)

The same holds for the mass of the current

{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
,

i.e.
,

|{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|\leq|(\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|,

and recalling also (
2.7
),

|{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|_{{\overline{C}}^{\varepsilon}_{l}}\leq|(\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{{\overline{C}}^{\varepsilon}_{l}}\leq|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{(D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}.

(5.9)

Remark 5.6
.

The area, counted with multiplicity,
of the
2
-rectifiable set

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
is
greater than or equal to the mass of the current
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
,
more specifically

\int_{D_{k}}|J(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})|~dx\geq|{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|_{\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\int_{D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})}|J(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})|~dx\geq|{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|_{\overline{C}^{\varepsilon}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})}.

(5.10)

This is due to the fact that

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
might overlap with opposite orientations
so that the multiplicity of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
vanishes,
and the overlappings do not contribute to its mass.
In particular,
{\rm spt}({\mathfrak{D}}_{k})\subseteq\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
.

From (
5.7
)
and (
5.10
)
it follows

|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{D_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geq|{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|_{\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})},\qquad|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{(D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geq|{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|_{\overline{C}_{l}^{\varepsilon}(1-\lambda_{k})}.

(5.11)

We now analyse the boundary of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
.
Up to small modifications, we will prove that it is boundaryless
in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})

(see (
5.20
) and (
5.23
), where
\lambda_{k}^{\prime}
are suitable small numbers in
(0,\lambda_{k})
chosen below in Definition
5.12
)
and so
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
can be symmetrized
according to Definition
3.3
. Before proceeding to the symmetrization
we need some preliminaries.
We build suitable currents

{\mathcal{W}}_{k}
,
with their support sets denoted by
W_{k}

(see (
5.17
) and (
5.16
)), with

\partial{\mathcal{W}}_{k}

coinciding with
\partial{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
(see (
5.21
),
(
5.22
), and (
5.23
)).

Remark 5.7
.

By (
5.4
),

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})
is contained in
\partial_{\rm lat}C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
.
By Lemma
4.2
(i),

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})
is the union of the image of
at most countably many curves,
and this union, counted with multiplicities, has
finite
\mathcal{H}^{1}

measure: specifically, if we
define

M(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})):=\int_{\partial D_{k}}\Big|\partial_{\rm tg}~(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})\Big|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

where

\partial_{\rm tg}

stands for the tangential derivative
along

\partial D_{k}
, then
M(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k}))<+\infty

since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial D_{k})<+\infty
(still by
Lemma
4.2
(i))
and
u_{k}
is Lipschitz continuous.

Moreover

\partial{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}=(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}{\in\mathcal{D}_{1}(C_{l})}\qquad{\rm in}~C_{l}.

(5.12)

It is convenient to introduce a suitable map

\tau
parametrizing the region

\overline{C}_{l}\setminus C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})

in between
the two concentric cylinders; this map can
then be pulled back by
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}
, but only in
\Omega\setminus D_{k}
,
to get the map
\widetilde{\tau}
.

Definition 5.8

(
The maps
\tau
,
\widetilde{\tau}
)
.

We set

\displaystyle\tau=\tau_{\lambda_{k}}:[1-\lambda_{k},1]\times\partial C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})\rightarrow\overline{C}_{l}\setminus C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})\subset\mathbb{R}^{3},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\tau(\rho,t,y):=\Big(t,\frac{y}{|y|}\rho\Big),\qquad\ \ \rho\in[1-\lambda_{k},1],\ \ \ (t,y)\in\partial C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})=[-1,l]\times\partial B_{1-\lambda_{k}}.

By (
5.2
) it follows

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus D_{k})\subset\partial C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
,
hence we can also set

\widetilde{\tau}(\rho,x)=\widetilde{\tau}_{u_{k},\lambda_{k}}(\rho,x):=\tau(\rho,\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(x)),\qquad\rho\in[1-\lambda_{k},1],\;x\in\Omega\setminus D_{k}.

(5.14)

Notice that
\tau(\rho,\cdot,\cdot)

takes values in
\partial C_{l}(\rho)
for any
\rho\in[1-\lambda_{k},1]
,
that
\tau(\cdot,t,y)
moves along the normal to the lateral boundary of

\partial C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
at the point
(t,y)
,
and
\tau(1-\lambda_{k},\cdot,\cdot)
is the identity. We also
observe that, due to the fact that
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}

takes values in
[0,l)\times\overline{B}_{1}
, the same holds for
\widetilde{\tau}
.

Remark 5.9
.

If
\lambda_{k}>0
is small enough
(which is true for
k
large enough),
the Jacobian of
\tau

is close to
1

so that the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measure, counted
with multiplicities,
of the set

\tau(\rho,\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k}))
is, for fixed
\rho

bounded by two times
the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measure
of
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})
,
still counted with multiplicities.
More precisely,

\displaystyle 2\int_{\partial D_{k}}\Big|\partial_{\rm tg}~(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})\Big|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}\geq\int_{\partial D_{k}}\Big|\partial_{\rm tg}~\tau(\rho,\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})\Big|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},\qquad\rho\in[1-\lambda_{k},1],

(5.15)

\displaystyle 2\int_{(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\cap\partial D_{k}}\Big|\partial_{\rm tg}~(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})\Big|~~d\mathcal{H}^{1}\geq\int_{(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\cap\partial D_{k}}\Big|\partial_{\rm tg}~\tau(\rho,\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})\Big|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

for all
\rho\in[1-\lambda_{k},1]
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough,
where we recall that, from Lemma
4.2
(i),

\partial D_{k}
is rectifiable.

Now we take a sequence
17
17

17

The sequence

(\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
depends on
\varepsilon
and
n
.
of numbers

, which will be fixed in the sequel

).

Definition 5.10

(
The set
W_{k}
and the current

{\mathcal{W}}_{k}
)
.

We define the

2
-rectifiable set
18
18

18

The set

consists of “vertical” walls, normal to

.

W_{k}:=\tau\big([1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})\big)=\widetilde{\tau}\big([1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times\partial D_{k}\big),

(5.16)

and the
2
-current

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{k}:=\widetilde{\tau}_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times\partial D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(C_{l}).

Clearly

;
Again, although

Remark 5.11

(
Use of
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\cdot}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
for not
top-dimensional currents
)
.

\partial D_{k}
is endowed with a natural orientation, inherited
from the fact that it is the boundary of the set
D_{k}
;
consistently, we sometimes use the identification

\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\partial D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
.
With a little abuse of notation we have noted the current
integration over
[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times\partial D_{k}
, meaning that
\partial D_{k}
is endowed with
this natural orientation.
Finally, recalling that
\tilde{\tau}(\rho,\cdot)
takes
values in
\partial C_{l}(\rho)
, we can do the following identification:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\tau}_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times\partial D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\widetilde{\tau}_{\sharp}\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\Big(C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})\Big).

We denote

M(W_{k}):=\int_{[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda^{\prime}_{k}]\times\partial D_{k}}|J(\widetilde{\tau}(\rho,x))|~d\rho~d{\mathcal{H}^{1}}(x)

(5.18)

the area of
W_{k}
counted
with multiplicities.
By the area formula and
using (
5.15
) we infer

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{W}}_{k}|\leq M(W_{k})\leq 2(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\int_{\partial D_{k}}\Big|\partial_{\rm tg}~(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})\Big|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=2(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})M(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})).

(5.19)

Then we are led to the following

Definition 5.12

(
The sequence
(\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
)
.

We select
\lambda_{k}^{\prime}\in(0,\lambda_{k})
so that

\displaystyle 2(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})M(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k}))\leq\frac{1}{n}\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.20)

Finally we observe that

\displaystyle\partial{\mathcal{W}}_{k}=\tau(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime},\cdot,\cdot)_{\sharp}\Big((\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\Big)-(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

(5.21)

Definition 5.13

(
The current
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
)
.

We define

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}:={\mathfrak{D}}_{k}+{\mathcal{W}}_{k}{\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(C_{l})}.

(5.22)

The next result will be useful to select a primitive of

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
.

Corollary 5.14
.

The current

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}

is supported
in
[0,l]\times\overline{B}_{1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}}

and

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\quad\textrm{is~ boundaryless~ in~the~open~cylinder~}C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}).

(5.23)

In particular

\partial\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}=0
in

\mathcal{D}_{1}((-\infty,l)\times B_{1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

The statement follows by construction, and
noticing that,
since
\tau(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime},\cdot,\cdot)_{\sharp}\Big((\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\Big)

has support in
\partial_{\rm lat}C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
,
one can use
(
5.12
) to deduce
(
5.23
).
∎

5.3
The
3
-current
\mathcal{E}_{k}

and the symmetrization of
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}

Since we want to symmetrize

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}

according to Definition
3.3
, we need to identify a unique
primitive
3
-current
\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that
\partial\mathcal{E}_{k}=\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
.

The restriction of the map
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}

to

\Omega\setminus D_{k}

takes

\Omega\setminus D_{k}
into

\partial C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
(see (
5.2
)),
and can also be written as

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(x)=\Big(|x|,\frac{u_{k}(x)}{|u_{k}(x)|}(1-\lambda_{k})\Big),\qquad x\in\Omega\setminus D_{k}.

(5.24)

The current
(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
has boundary

\displaystyle\partial(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=-(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

(5.25)

Definition 5.15

(
The currents
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
and
\mathcal{X}_{k}
)
.

Recalling the definition of
\tau
(see (
5.14
), (
5.13
)) we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{k}:=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\tau}_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times(\Omega\setminus D_{k})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(C_{l}),

(5.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{k}:=

\displaystyle\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}-\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(C_{l}).

(5.27)

Notice that
\mathcal{X}_{k}
cannot be directly defined as a push-forward
via the map
\widetilde{\tau}
, for
part of
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
could be contained in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
,
and for this reason
we are led to define it as a difference.

The current
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
could have multiplicity
different from
0
and
1
, and in particular could
not be the integration over a finite perimeter set.
This depends on the fact that the map
\Psi_{k}
could generate
overlappings and
self-intersections of the set
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus D_{k})
.
If the multiplicity of
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
is only
1
or
0
then the same holds
for
\mathcal{X}_{k}
.
Also,
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
might be null, and in this case
\mathcal{X}_{k}

coincides with the integration over the region

C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
.
A finer description of these two currents will be necessary later,
and this will be done by a slicing argument in Lemma
6.4
below.

Recalling (
5.17
),

\displaystyle\partial\mathcal{Y}_{k}=-{\mathcal{W}}_{k}=-\partial\mathcal{X}_{k}\qquad\text{in }\ C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k}),

as it can be seen by considering the push-forward by
\tau
of (
5.25
).
We proceed to the symmetrization in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})

of the current

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
in (
5.22
).
By (
5.23
)
it follows the existence of an integer multiplicity
3
-current
\mathcal{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{3}(C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}))
such that

\displaystyle\partial\mathcal{E}_{k}=\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\qquad{\rm in}~\ C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}).

(5.28)

The current
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is unique up to a constant, that we might assume to be integer, since
\mathcal{E}_{k}
has integer multiplicity.
Hence we choose such a constant
19
19

19

The fact that this choice is possible is a
consequence of the
constancy theorem (see for instance

[
23
, Proposition 7.3.1]
).
Indeed, let
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}
have the same boundary
(
i.e.
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{k}
)
of
\mathcal{X}_{k}
in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})
.
Thus
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}-\mathcal{X}_{k}
is boundaryless,
and must be an integer multiple of the integration
over
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})
,
i.e.
,

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}-\mathcal{X}_{k}=h\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
. We then set
\mathcal{E}_{k}:=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{k}-h\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
so that
\mathcal{E}_{k}=\mathcal{X}_{k}
in
{C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})}
.

so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\Big(C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})\Big)=\mathcal{X}_{k}.

(5.29)

Let
E_{k}
denote
the support of
\mathcal{E}_{k}
; by decomposition,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=\sum_{i}(-1)^{\sigma_{i}}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{E_{k,i}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\qquad\text{ in }\ C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}),

(5.30)

with
E_{k,i}\subset C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
finite perimeter sets, the decomposition
done with undecomposable components, see (
3.3
), (
3.4
). We denote

\mathbb{S}(E_{k}):=\cup_{i}\mathbb{S}(E_{k,i})

the union of the cylindrical symmetrizations of the sets
E_{k,i}
,
see (
3.2
).
Recalling
(
5.28
),
Definition

3.3

and (
3.5
),
the symmetrization of the current
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})=\partial\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{E}_{k})=\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\mathbb{S}(E_{k})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}).

(5.31)

Formula (
5.31
)
contains the needed information about the symmetrization of
\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
,
since by construction
{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}=\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})
(recall (
5.6
)).

We have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}=\sum_{i}(-1)^{\sigma_{i}}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\partial^{*}E_{k,i}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}\qquad\text{ in }C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}),

(5.32)

and since the decomposition in (
5.30
) is
done by undecomposable components, by (

) it follows, in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|=\sum_{i}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial^{*}E_{k,i})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\partial^{*}E_{k,i}\subseteq{\rm spt}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}).

Remark 5.16

(
Nonuniqueness of the decomposition
)
.

Once the decomposition (
5.30
) is fixed, the symmetrization is uniquely determined. However, the decomposition might not be unique, and the resulting symmetrized current in general depends on the choice of the decomposition. This will not be an issue,
since our procedure will lead to a minimization problem which will not depend on this step.

Since

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial^{*}\mathbb{S}(E_{k,i}))\leq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial^{*}E_{k,i})\qquad\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{N},

and
\mathbb{S}(E_{k})=\cup_{i}\mathbb{S}(E_{k,i})
, we also have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial^{*}\mathbb{S}(E_{k}))\leq\sum_{i}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\partial^{*}E_{k,i})=|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|.

(5.33)

The same inequalities hold if we restrict the mass to the set
C^{\varepsilon}_{l}
,
namely

|\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})|_{C^{\varepsilon}_{l}}\leq|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}|_{C^{\varepsilon}_{l}}.

(5.34)

Now we want to understand whether
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
has some boundary on
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We have already observed (Corollary
5.14
) that
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}
has no boundary in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
. The same holds for the symmetrized current:

Corollary 5.17

(
Closedness of

\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
in
(-\infty,l)\times B_{1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}}
)
.

The current
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
is supported in
[0,l]\times\overline{B}_{1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}}
and

\partial\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})=0
in

\mathcal{D}_{1}((-\infty,l)\times B_{1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
is the
boundary of the current carried by the integration
over the finite perimeter set
\mathbb{S}(E_{k})
in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
. Hence
\partial\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})=0

in
\mathcal{D}_{1}(C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}))
.
The conclusion then follows from the fact that
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})

is supported in
[0,l)\times B_{1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}}\subset C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
.
∎

6
Towards an estimate of
|\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})|
: two
useful lemmas

Now that the symmetrization
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
of the current

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}

in

C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
is obtained
(see (

)),
we need to estimate its mass.
This will be done separately
in

and in

In formula (

) of Section

we express the restriction of

to

as generalized graph of

and

and estimate the area of these graphs

, below).
In addition, we need a fine description of the

on

;

.

We start by collecting
in Lemma

and Lemma

two important preliminary estimates;
we need to introduce the functions
|u_{k}|^{-}
,
|u_{k}|^{+}
.

For any
r\in(\varepsilon,l)

we consider the
closed curve
20
20

20

We use here polar coordinates
(r,\alpha)
.

\alpha\in(0,2\pi]\mapsto\Psi_{k}(r,\alpha)\in\{r\}\times\overline{B}_{1}
;
the image of
\Psi_{k}(r,\cdot)

is the slice
of
\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})
with the plane

.

Definition 6.1

(
The functions
|u_{k}|^{\pm}
)
.

For all
r\in(\varepsilon,l)
we define

|u_{k}|^{-}(r):=\min_{\alpha\in(0,2\pi]}|u_{k}(r,\alpha)|,\qquad|u_{k}|^{+}(r):=\max_{\alpha\in(0,2\pi]}|u_{k}(r,\alpha)|,

(6.1)

Thus the map
\Psi_{k}(r,\cdot)
defined in (
5.1
) takes values in

\{r\}\times(\overline{B}_{|u_{k}|^{+}(r)}\setminus B_{|u_{k}|^{-}(r)}).

Let us remark that
|u_{k}|^{-}(r)
might be equal to
0
,
that
|u_{k}|^{+}(r)\leq 1
,
and that it might happen that

|u_{k}|^{+}(r)=|u_{k}|^{-}(r)
,
see Fig.
4
.
Moreover, from
(
5.4
),

|u_{k}|\geq 1-\lambda_{k}\qquad\text{in}~\Omega\setminus D_{k},

(6.2)

so that

\displaystyle|u_{k}|^{+}(r)\geq 1-\lambda_{k}\qquad\text{ if ~}r~\text{is~such~that~}\ (\Omega\setminus D_{k})\cap\partial{\rm B}_{r}\neq\mathchar 31\relax,

whereas it might happen that

\displaystyle|u_{k}|^{+}(r)<1-\lambda_{k}\qquad\text{ if ~}r~\text{is~such~that~}\ (\Omega\setminus D_{k})\cap\partial{\rm B}_{r}=\mathchar 31\relax.

(6.3)

In such a case, since

D_{k}\subseteq A_{n}
(Lemma
4.2
(ii)),
this can happen only if
\partial{\rm B}_{r}\subseteq A_{n}
.

Definition 6.2

(
The set
Q_{k,\varepsilon}
)
.

We define

Q_{k,\varepsilon}:=\{r\in(\varepsilon,l):|u_{k}|^{+}(r)<1-\lambda_{k}\}.

(6.4)

Then

Q_{k,\varepsilon}\subseteq\{r\in(\varepsilon,l):\partial{\rm B}_{r}\subseteq A_{n}\}.

(6.5)

The next lemma, that will be used in
Section

9
,
shows that the measure of

is small
(see Fig.
4
).

Lemma 6.3

(
Estimate of
Q_{k,\varepsilon}
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(Q_{k,\varepsilon})<\frac{1}{2\pi\varepsilon n}.

Proof.

If
t\in Q_{k,\varepsilon}
then
\partial{\rm B}_{t}\subseteq A_{n}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(Q_{k,\varepsilon})=\int_{Q_{k,\varepsilon}}1dt\leq\frac{1}{2\pi\varepsilon}\int_{Q_{k,\varepsilon}}2\pi t~dt=\frac{1}{2\pi\varepsilon}\int_{Q_{k,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{\rm B}_{t})dt\leq\frac{1}{2\pi\varepsilon}|A_{n}|,

where the last inequality is a consequence of the coarea
formula and (
6.5
).
The thesis then follows recalling that

|A_{n}|<\frac{1}{n}
, see (
4.6
).
∎

Figure 4:
The graphs of the functions
|u_{k}|^{+}
and

defined in (

),
and the set

in (

By slicing and from (

),
(

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}_{k})_{t,\rho}=\sum_{i}(-1)^{\sigma_{i}}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{E_{k,i}\cap(\{t\}\times\partial B_{\rho})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{S}(E_{k})\cap(\{t\}\times\partial B_{\rho}))\leq\sum_{i}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{k,i}\cap(\{t\}\times\partial B_{\rho}))=|(\mathcal{X}_{k})_{t,\rho}|,

(6.6)

since the decomposition is done in undecomposable components (see

Recalling the definition of

denotes the measure (in radiants) of the slice

.
By construction,

\displaystyle\Theta_{k}(t,\rho)=\Theta_{k}(t,\varrho)\qquad\text{for any }\rho,\varrho\in(1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}),

since the slices of

\mathcal{X}_{k}
, and hence of the sets

, are
radially symmetric
21
21

21

Each radial section is (suitably rescaled) the
same since, by definition, function

in (

)
is radial.
in

C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\setminus\overline{C_{l}}(1-\lambda_{k})
.
Also, the right-hand side of (

We now look

Lemma 6.4

(
L^{1}
-estimate of the angular slices
)
.

We have

\int_{\varepsilon}^{l}\Theta_{k}(t,\rho)~dt\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon n}+o_{k}(1)\qquad\forall\rho\in(1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}),

(6.8)

where

is a nonnegative function, depending on

and

,
and infinitesimal as
k\rightarrow+\infty
.

Proof.

It is convenient to set

\displaystyle H_{k,t}:=D_{k}\cap\partial{\rm B}_{t},\qquad H_{k,t}^{c}:=(\Omega\setminus D_{k})\cap\partial{\rm B}_{t}\qquad\forall t\in(\varepsilon,l).

(6.9)

Before passing to case (C), we anticipate an observation which will be useful to deal with it.
Let
\overline{x_{1}x_{2}}\subset(\Omega\setminus D_{k})\cap\partial{\rm B}_{t}
be an arc oriented counterclockwise. We want to identify the current
(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{x_{1}x_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
; to do that we consider three different cases for
\overline{x_{1}x_{2}}
. Case 1:
|\frac{x_{1}}{|x_{1}|}-\frac{x_{2}}{|x_{2}|}|>2\lambda_{k}

and

(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{x_{1}x_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{z_{1}z_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

\overline{z_{1}z_{2}}
is the arc connecting
z_{1},z_{2}
, starting from
z_{1}
and oriented counterclockwise. Case 2:
|\frac{x_{1}}{|x_{1}|}-\frac{x_{2}}{|x_{2}|}|\leq 2\lambda_{k}
(that implies
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\leq 4\lambda_{k}
, and

could have reversed order of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
). Let

have the same order of
x_{1},x_{2}
, then
(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{x_{1}x_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{z_{1}z_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
where
\overline{z_{1}z_{2}}
is the arc connecting
z_{1},z_{2}
, starting from
z_{1}
and oriented counterclockwise. Now let

have the reversed order of
x_{1},x_{2}
. If

is the short path arc connecting

, then

(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{x_{1}x_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{z_{1}z_{2}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

,

is oriented counterclockwise, and

is the (short path) arc starting from

and oriented counterclockwise. Notice also that in case 2 we always have

.

We are now in a position to specify the arc:

29

29

Likewise

.
from

Once we have specified the image arc,
we can define
\widehat{P}_{k,i}:\overline{x_{2}^{i}x_{1}^{i+1}}\rightarrow\overline{z^{i}_{2}z^{i+1}_{1}}

We then introduce

\displaystyle P_{k}(x):=\begin{cases}\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(x)&\text{if }x\in H^{c}_{k,t},\\
\\
\widehat{P}_{k,i}(x)&\text{if }x\in\overline{x_{2}^{i}x_{1}^{i+1}}\text{~for some }i.\end{cases}

We claim that

\displaystyle\tau(\rho,P_{k}(\cdot))_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\partial{\rm B}_{t}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\{t\}\times\partial B_{\rho}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

Since the map
\tau(\rho,\cdot)
is an orientation preserving
homeomorphism between
\partial B_{1-\lambda_{k}}
and
\partial B_{\rho}
, it is sufficient to show that

\displaystyle P_{k}(\cdot)_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\partial{\rm B}_{t}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\{t\}\times\partial B_{1-\lambda_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

(6.18)

Equivalently, we will prove that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{h}(\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{z_{1}^{i}z_{2}^{i}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}+\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{z_{2}^{i}z_{1}^{i+1}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}})=\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\{t\}\times\partial B_{1-\lambda_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

Let
\omega_{i}
(resp.

\beta_{i}
) be the angular amplitude,
in counterclockwise order, of the arc
\overline{x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{i}}
(resp.

\overline{x_{2}^{i}x_{1}^{i+1}}
). Trivially we have

\sum_{i=1}^{h}(\omega_{i}+\beta_{i})=2\pi
.
If
\widehat{\omega}_{i}
(resp.
\widehat{\beta}_{i}
) is
the angular amplitude of
\overline{z_{1}^{i}z_{2}^{i}}

(resp.
\overline{z_{2}^{i}z_{1}^{i+1}}
),
taken with sign
\pm 1
according to their orientation, we
see that to prove (

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{h}(\widehat{\omega}_{i}+\widehat{\beta}_{i})=2\pi.

(6.20)

To do this we use (
6.15
); notice first

) holds for

and

: Namely

transforms

,

of amplitude

in such a way that

\displaystyle|\widehat{\omega}_{i}-\omega_{i}|\leq 2\widehat{\lambda}_{k}.

(6.21)

Now, if
\theta_{j}^{i}
is the angular coordinate of
z_{j}^{i}
, and

\alpha^{i}_{j}
is the angular coordinate of
x_{j}^{i}
,
we know that

\theta_{j}^{i}=\alpha_{j}^{i}+r_{j}^{i},\qquad\text{ with }|r_{j}^{i}|\leq\widehat{\lambda}_{k}.

(6.22)

Here again
\widehat{\lambda}_{k}
is the angle of a chord of length
\lambda_{k}
on

\partial B_{1-\lambda_{k}}
.
To prove (
6.20
) we reduce ourselves to show that

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{i}=\omega_{i}+r_{2}^{i}-r_{1}^{i},

(6.23)

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{i}=\beta_{i}+r_{1}^{i+1}-r_{2}^{i},

(6.24)

for all
i
.
Fix
i
; we can assume
\alpha_{2}^{i}=\alpha_{1}^{i}+\omega_{i}
, and by (
6.22
) we get

\widehat{\omega}_{i}=\omega_{i}+r_{2}^{i}-r_{1}^{i}+2k_{i}\pi,

with

accordingly to the number of oriented complete turns
around the circle
\partial B_{1-\lambda_{k}}
. From (

) we have

for all

, and (
6.23
) follows.
A similar argument,
using (
6.15
),
leads to (
6.24
), hence (
6.20
) is proved, and (
6.17
) follows at once.

Define

y_{j}^{i}:=\tau(\rho,z_{j}^{i})\in\{t\}\times\partial B_{\rho}.

From (
6.17
),
(
6.11
), and (
6.12
) it follows that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}_{k})_{t,\rho}=\tau(\rho,P_{k}(\cdot))_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\partial{\rm B}_{t}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}-\tau(\rho,\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\cdot))_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{H_{k,t}^{c}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=\tau(\rho,P_{k}(\cdot))_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{H_{k,t}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}},

(6.25)

so that,
since the maps
\tau(\rho,\cdot)
send the arcs
\overline{z^{i}_{2}z^{i+1}_{1}}
onto
\overline{y^{i}_{2}y^{i+1}_{1}}
,
we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}_{k})_{t,\rho}=\sum_{i=1}^{h}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\overline{y_{2}^{i}y_{1}^{i+1}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}},

(6.26)

hence

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{X}_{k})_{t,\rho}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{h}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\overline{y_{2}^{i}y_{1}^{i+1}}).

(6.27)

We now estimate the length of the arcs
\overline{y_{2}^{i}y_{1}^{i+1}}
.
For simplicity we fix
i
and
set
Y_{1}:=y_{2}^{i}
,
Y_{2}:=y_{1}^{i+1}
,

X_{1}:=x_{2}^{i}
and
X_{2}:=x_{1}^{i+1}
.
Let
{\rm d}(\cdot,\cdot)
denote the distance
between points of

\{t\}\times\partial B_{\rho}
(
i.e.
, the length of the minimal arc connecting the two points), let
\pi_{\rho}
be the orthogonal
projection of
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\rm target}

onto the convex set
\overline{B}_{\rho}
,
and write

Y_{i}=(t,\widetilde{Y}_{i})

with
\widetilde{Y}_{i}\in\overline{B}_{\rho}
,
for
i=1,2
.
Then, setting
\widehat{X}_{i}:=\frac{X_{i}}{|X_{i}|}

and denoting

\overline{\widehat{X}_{1}\widehat{X}_{2}}
the arc between

\widehat{X}_{1}
and
\widehat{X}_{2}
on

\{t\}\times\partial B_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(\overline{Y_{1}Y_{2}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}\Big(\overline{\pi_{\rho}(X_{1})\pi_{\rho}(X_{2})}\Big)+{\rm d}(\pi_{\rho}(X_{1}),\widetilde{Y}_{1})+{\rm d}(\pi_{\rho}(X_{2}),\widetilde{Y}_{2})

(6.28)

\displaystyle=\rho\mathcal{H}^{1}\Big(\overline{\widehat{X}_{1}\widehat{X}_{2}}\Big)+{\rm d}(\pi_{\rho}(X_{1}),\widetilde{Y}_{1})+{\rm d}(\pi_{\rho}(X_{2}),\widetilde{Y}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\rho\mathcal{H}^{1}\Big(\overline{\widehat{X}_{1}\widehat{X}_{2}}\Big)+\frac{\pi}{2}|\pi_{\rho}(X_{1})-\widetilde{Y}_{1}|+\frac{\pi}{2}|\pi_{\rho}(X_{2})-\widetilde{Y}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\rho\mathcal{H}^{1}\Big(\overline{\widehat{X}_{1}\widehat{X}_{2}}\Big)+\frac{\pi}{2}|\pi_{\rho}(X_{1})-\pi_{\rho}\circ u_{k}(X_{1})|+\frac{\pi}{2}|\pi_{\rho}\circ u_{k}(X_{1})-\widetilde{Y}_{1}|

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi}{2}|\pi_{\rho}(X_{2})-\pi_{\rho}\circ u_{k}(X_{2})|+\frac{\pi}{2}|\pi_{\rho}\circ u_{k}(X_{2})-\widetilde{Y}_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{H}^{1}\Big(\overline{X_{1}X_{2}}\Big)+\frac{\pi}{2}\big(d_{k}(X_{1})+d_{k}(X_{2})\big)+\pi(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}),

where we use that, for
x\neq 0
,

d_{k}(x)=|\frac{x}{|x|}-u_{k}(x)|=|u(x)-u_{k}(x)|\geq|\pi_{\rho}\circ u(x)-\pi_{\rho}\circ u_{k}(x)|,

because
{\rm Lip}(\pi_{\rho})=1
,

|\pi_{\rho}\circ u_{k}(X_{i})-\widetilde{Y}_{i}|\leq\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}

for
i=1,2
, and
X_{i}\in\partial{\rm B}_{t}
,
t>\varepsilon
.
By (
6.10
)
(
6.27
)
and (
6.28
), we infer

\displaystyle\Theta_{k}(t,\rho)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{H}^{1}(H_{k,t})+\frac{\pi}{2\rho}\sum_{x\in\partial H_{k,t}}(d_{k}(x)+\lambda_{k}).

(6.29)

Estimate (
6.29
) holds for
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-almost
every
t\in(\varepsilon,l)

such that neither case (A) nor (B) happens. Moreover, by (
6.13
) it holds also in case (A).
Case (B) does not contribute to the
L^{1}
norm of
\Theta_{k}(\cdot,\rho)
,
and therefore (
6.29
) holds for
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-almost every
t\in(\varepsilon,l)
.

Denoting by
m(x)=|x|
, so that
|\nabla m|=1
out of the origin, the coarea formula allows us to write

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{k}}d_{k}(\sigma)d\mathcal{H}^{1}(\sigma)\geq\int_{\partial D_{k}}|\frac{\partial m}{\partial\sigma}|d_{k}(\sigma)d\mathcal{H}^{1}(\sigma)=\int_{\varepsilon}^{l}\sum_{x\in m^{-1}(t)\cap\partial D_{k}}d_{k}(x)dt=\int_{\varepsilon}^{l}\sum_{x\in\partial H_{k,t}}d_{k}(x)dt.

Similarly

\displaystyle\int_{\partial D_{k}}\lambda_{k}~d\mathcal{H}^{1}(\sigma)\geq\int_{\varepsilon}^{l}\lambda_{k}\mathcal{H}^{0}(\{x\in\partial H_{k,t}\})~dt.

Recalling (
4.6
), from (
6.29
) we
finally get

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{l}\Theta_{k}(t,\rho)dt\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}|D_{k}|+\frac{\pi}{2(1-\lambda_{k})}\int_{\partial D_{k}}\left(d_{k}(\sigma)+2\lambda_{k}\right)~d\mathcal{H}^{1}(\sigma)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon n}+o_{k}(1),

where
o_{k}(1)
depends on
\varepsilon
and
n
(since
\lambda_{k}
does) and
vanishes as
k\rightarrow+\infty
, thanks to Lemma

4.2
(iii).
∎

7
Estimate from below of the mass of
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

over
D_{k}\cap(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})

Now we want to identify the current
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
in
(
5.31
)
as
sum of polar graphs (Section
2.4
), and
to do this we need some preliminaries.

Definition 7.1

(
The function
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
)
.

Recalling the definition of
\Theta_{k}=\Theta_{\mathbb{S}(E_{k})}
in (
6.7
),
we set

\displaystyle\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}:(\varepsilon,l)\times(0,1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times\{0\}\rightarrow[0,\pi],\qquad\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}(t,\rho,0):=\frac{\Theta_{k}(t,\rho)}{2}.

(7.1)

Note that
{\rm dom}(\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon})\subsetneq{\rm dom}(\Theta_{k})
.
The polar graph of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
is the set

G^{{\rm pol}}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}=\{(t,\rho,\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}(t,\rho,0)):(t,\rho,0)\in(\varepsilon,l)\times(0,1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times\{0\}\}
.
By construction
\mathbb{S}(E_{k})
is
the polar subgraph of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
restricted to the half-cylinder

\{(t,\rho,\theta):t\in(\varepsilon,l),\theta\in(0,\pi)\}
.
More precisely, let
\eta
be any number
30
30

30

\eta=0
is not allowed,
since in this case the boundary of the subgraph (as a current) does
not include the set where
\theta=0
.

with
0<\eta<\frac{\pi}{4}
;
then the polar subgraph

SG^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}:=\{(t,\rho,\theta)\in(\varepsilon,l)\times(0,1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times(-\pi/4,\pi):\theta\in(-\eta,\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}(t,\rho,0))\}

satisfies

\displaystyle SG^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}\cap\{\theta\in(0,\pi)\}=\mathbb{S}(E_{k})\cap\{\theta\in(0,\pi)\},

(7.2)

and similarly (for the polar epigraph), setting

UG^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}:=\{(t,\rho,\theta)\in(\varepsilon,l)\times(0,1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times(-\pi,\pi/4):\theta\in(-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}(t,\rho,0),\eta)\},

we have

\displaystyle UG^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}\cap\{\theta\in(-\pi,0)\}=\mathbb{S}(E_{k})\cap\{\theta\in(-\pi,0)\}.

(7.3)

Remark 7.2

(
The sets
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}=0
,
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}=\pi
)
.

Careful attention must be paid to the sets

\{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}=0\}
and
\{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}=\pi\}
. Indeed on such sets the
two graphs of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
and
-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
overlap and then, when
considered as integral currents, they cancel each other.
Moreover the set
\partial^{*}\mathbb{S}(E_{k})

includes the two graphs of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
and
-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
with the exception of these two sets.
In other words, from (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}(E_{k})\cap C^{\varepsilon}_{l}=\big(SG^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}\cap\{\theta\in(0,\pi)\}\big)\cup\big(UG^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}\cap\{\theta\in(-\pi,0)\}\big)

(7.4)

up to
\mathcal{H}^{3}
-negligible sets.
From this formula it is evident that the graphs of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
and
-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
over

\{\Theta_{k}=0\}\cup\{\Theta_{k}=2\pi\}
cancel each other,
and thus they do not belong to the reduced boundary of
\mathbb{S}(E_{k})
.
Moreover, the polar subgraph and the polar epigraph are sets
of finite perimeter, as is their union in (
7.4
).

Definition 7.3

(
The polar projection map
\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}
)
.

We let

\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}=\pi^{{\rm pol}}_{0,\lambda_{k}^{\prime},\varepsilon}:\overline{C}_{l}^{\varepsilon}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\rightarrow\overline{C}_{l}^{\varepsilon}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\cap\{\theta=0\}
be the polar projection defined by

\displaystyle\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}(t,\rho,\theta):=(t,\rho,0).

(7.5)

We now introduce various subsets of

(0,l)\times(0,1)\times\{0\}

in cylindrical coordinates, namely

\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))\subseteq S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\subseteq S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cup J_{Q_{k,\varepsilon}}\subseteq S^{(4)}_{k,\varepsilon}

We start with

(see also formulas (

\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))

coincides with

Remark 7.5
.

(i)

It might happen that
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})=\mathchar 31\relax
. By construction we have

\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))\cap\Big(((\varepsilon,l)\setminus Q_{k,\varepsilon})\times[1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times\{0\}\Big)=((\varepsilon,l)\setminus Q_{k,\varepsilon})\times\{1-\lambda_{k}\}\times\{0\}.

The two functions
|u_{k}|^{-}
and
|u_{k}|^{+}
could coincide in large portions
of
(\varepsilon,l)
(and even
everywhere), so that
\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}))

could collapse to a curve (for instance if
\Psi_{k}(\Omega)\subset\partial C_{l}
);
see also the example in Section
10.1
.
On the other hand,

\mathcal{H}^{2}(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon})>0
(see Lemma
6.3
).

(ii)

Notice that
A\supseteq\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k}\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\cup\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}((\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\setminus D_{k})
.
Moreover
A\cap\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})
may not be empty.

(iii)

Inside the cylinder
C^{\varepsilon}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k})
,

S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}
is exactly the
\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}
-projection of

\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})
;
remember also that
(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}(\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}})=(\partial\mathcal{E}_{k})\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits C_{l}^{\varepsilon}(1-\lambda_{k})
, by (
5.6
), (
5.22
)
and (
5.28
).

(iv)

Recalling the definition of

in (
5.16
),

\displaystyle\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}\Big(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon})\cup W_{k}\Big)\subseteq S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon},

(7.8)

and the above inclusion might be strict.

(v)

If
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}(t,\rho,0)\in(0,\pi)
then
(t,\rho,0)\in S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}
, by (
7.8
). Indeed in this case the
circle
(\pi_{0}^{\rm pol})^{-1}(t,\rho,0)
intersects both some sets in
\{E_{k,i}\}
(see (
5.30
)) and their complement, so in particular

must intersect the reduced boundary of some of the sets in

,
namely

, for
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-a.e.
(t,\rho,0)\in S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}
. Furthermore

\displaystyle\{(t,\rho,0):\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}(t,\rho,0)\in(0,\pi)\}=\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}\big({\rm spt}(\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}))\big),

up to
\mathcal{H}^{2}-
negligible sets
31
31

31

There could be
(t,\rho,0)\in\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}\big({\rm spt}(\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}))\big)
such that

.
Indeed take

and

and

-set.

Remark 7.6
.

on

7.1
The current
{\mathbb{S}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})

as sum of a polar subgraph and a polar epigraph

Let
G^{\rm pol}_{\pm\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}

be the polar graph of
\pm\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)
; these two sets, by symmetry, overlap,
and

\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}+\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}=0,

due to the fact that
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

and
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
are oriented in
opposite way. Indeed we endow
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
with the orientation inherited by looking at it as the
boundary of the polar subgraph of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
, and we endow
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
with
the opposite orientation,
since we look at it as boundary of an epigraph.

Definition 7.7

(
The currents
{\mathcal{G}}^{\pm}_{k,\varepsilon}
)
.

We
set

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{k,\varepsilon}:=(\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{SG^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}})\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\big(\{\theta\in(0,\pi)\}\cap C^{\varepsilon}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\big)+\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}},

(7.9)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{-}:=(\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{UG^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}})\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\big(\{\theta\in(-\pi,0)\}\cap C^{\varepsilon}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})\big)+\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G^{\rm pol}_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\left(S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}\cap\{\Theta_{k}\in\{0,2\pi\}\}\right)}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}.

The non standard orientation of
{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{-}

is chosen in such a way that condition (
7.10
)
in Proposition
7.9
below takes place.
In this proposition we will also see that, being
\mathbb{S}(E_{k})
a finite perimeter set in
C_{l}^{\varepsilon}
, its
reduced boundary, seen as a current, has finite mass. In turn, the integration on its boundary is exactly

{\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{k,\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{G}}_{k,\varepsilon}^{-}
(see also (
7.4
)).

Remark 7.8
.

The generalized polar graph of
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}
might have large parts on which
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\in\{0,\pi\}
;
for this reason we neglected this part in the currents introduced in (
7.9
) by
restricting the boundary of the subgraph
in
\{\theta\in(0,\pi)\}\cap C^{\varepsilon}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
(and similarly for the epigraph). However we want to consider the graph above the set
\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\in\{0,\pi\}
on the strip
S^{(2)}_{k,\varepsilon}
, in particular the
projection of the set where
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})

to the measure of

As a consequence of (

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{H}^{2}(J_{k,\varepsilon}^{0,2\pi})

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(\pi_{0}^{\rm pol}(Z_{k}^{0}\cap C^{\varepsilon}_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}))\big)+2(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})l

Lemma 7.13
.

The following assertions hold:

(i)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(3)}|=|{\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{k,\varepsilon}|+\mathcal{H}^{2}(J_{Q_{k,\varepsilon}});

Proof.

and

have disjoint supports, and

and
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{-\vartheta_{k,\varepsilon}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits J_{Q_{k,\varepsilon}}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

37

Looking at the plane

.

Proof.

,
the orientation of
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\Sigma_{k,\varepsilon}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
is the one inherited by the external
normal to
\partial\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{\mathbb{S}(E_{k})}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}
, namely the outward
unit normal to the lateral
boundary of
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
.
∎

8
Estimate from below of the mass of
\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}

over
D_{k}\cap{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}

We now analyse the image of
D_{k}\cap{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}
through
\Psi_{k}
.
We want to reduce this set to a current
\mathcal{V}_{k}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\{\varepsilon\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
(defined in (
8.12
)), in order that it contains the necessary information on the area of
\Psi_{k}(D_{k}\cap{\rm B}_{\varepsilon})
. To this aim we need first to describe the boundary of
\mathcal{V}_{k}
and then show that
its mass gives a lower bound for the area of the graph
of
u_{k}
(see formula (
8.13
)).

Borrowing the notation from the proof of Lemma
6.4
,
the set
\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}

is splitted as:

\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}=(D_{k}\cap\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon})\cup((\Omega\setminus D_{k})\cap\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon})=:H_{k,\varepsilon}\cup H_{k,\varepsilon}^{c}.

(8.1)

We denote by

\displaystyle\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{I_{k}}\subseteq\{\widehat{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{J_{k}}:=\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}\cap\partial D_{k},

(8.2)

the finite family of points
(see Lemma
4.2
(v)) which
represents the relative boundary of
H_{k,\varepsilon}
in

\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}
.
Recall that
\{\widehat{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{J_{k}}
is finite
as well by Lemma
4.2
(iv). For notational simplicity,
we skip the dependence on
\epsilon
.

Recalling the definition of
W_{k}
in (
5.16
),
the following crucial lemma states that

(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}))\cup W_{k}
does not intersect the plane
\{\varepsilon\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

in a set of positive
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-measure.

Lemma 8.1
.

The rectifiable set
(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}))\cup{W_{k}}

satisfies

\mathcal{H}^{2}\Big(\big(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\cup W_{k}\big)\cap\{t=\varepsilon\}\Big)=0.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that
\mathcal{H}^{2}((\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k}))\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})=0
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}({W_{k}}\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})=0
. To show the
first equality, suppose

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})>0
.
Since
{\rm Lip}(\pi_{\lambda_{k}})=1

and
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}
takes on the plane
\{t=\varepsilon\}
into itself, we have

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})>0
. Again,
being
\Psi_{k}
Lipschitz continuous, we deduce that

\Psi_{k}^{-1}(\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})
has positive measure.
But
\Psi_{k}^{-1}(\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})\subset\Psi_{k}^{-1}(\{t=\varepsilon\})=\partial{\rm B}_{\varepsilon}
which has obviously
\mathcal{H}^{2}
null measure.

Let us prove that
\mathcal{H}^{2}({W_{k}}\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})=0
.
Recalling (see (
5.16
))
that
W_{k}=\tau([1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times\gamma_{k})

with
\gamma_{k}:=\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})
,
and since
\tau(\cdot,z)
in (
5.13
)
does not change the axial coordinate
of
z
, we see
38
38

38

For instance, using the coarea formula.
that
\tau([1-\lambda_{k},1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}]\times\gamma_{k})\cap\{t=\varepsilon\}
has positive
\mathcal{H}^{2}
measure only
if
\gamma_{k}\cap\{t=\varepsilon\}
has positive
\mathcal{H}^{1}
measure.
Again, since also
\pi_{\lambda_{k}}
does not change the axial coordinate,
as before this happens only if
\Psi_{k}^{-1}(\widehat{\gamma}_{k}\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})
has positive
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measure, where
\widehat{\gamma}_{k}:=\Psi_{k}(\partial D_{k})
; by Lemma
4.2
, this is not possible, since
we know that
\widehat{\gamma}_{k}\cap\{t=\varepsilon\}=\{\Psi_{k}(\widehat{x}_{i})\}
(see (
8.2
)), and then
\Psi_{k}^{-1}(\widehat{\gamma}_{k}\cap\{t=\varepsilon\})=\{\widehat{x}_{i}\}
which is a finite set.
∎

We recall from (
5.6
) and (
5.22
) that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}=(\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k})_{\sharp}\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{D_{k}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}+{\mathcal{W}}_{k}.

(8.3)

An immediate consequence of Lemma
8.1
,
formula (
8.3
), and the fact that
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}

is boundaryless in
C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime})
, is the following:

Corollary 8.2
.

We have
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\{t=\varepsilon\}=0
. In particular

\displaystyle\partial(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\{\varepsilon<t<l\})\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits(\{t=\varepsilon\})=-\partial(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\{-1<t<\varepsilon\})\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits(\{t=\varepsilon\})\qquad\text{ in }C_{l}(1-\lambda_{k}^{\prime}).

If
\{E_{k,i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
E_{k,i}\in C_{l}
are the sets which we
have symmetrized (see (
5.30
)),
\mathbb{S}(E_{k})
is the symmetrized set, and
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
is the symmetrized current,
we have to understand the behaviour of
\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})
on
\{\varepsilon\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We have observed that

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits(\{\varepsilon\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})=0

because
\mathcal{H}^{2}\left((\pi_{\lambda_{k}}\circ\Psi_{k}(D_{k})\cup W_{k})\cap\{\varepsilon\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)=0
.
The same holds for the symmetrized current, as a particular consequence
of Lemma
3.4
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}(\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{k})\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits(\{\varepsilon\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})=0.
