), it follows

|D^{s}\psi_{k,\varepsilon}|(R_{l})=|D^{s}\psi_{k,\varepsilon}|\big(\overline{R}_{l}\setminus(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\})\big)

and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi_{k,\varepsilon},R_{l})=\mathbb{A}\big(\psi_{k,\varepsilon},\overline{R}_{l}\setminus(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\})\big).

Recalling the expression (
11.1
) of

\partial_{D}R_{l}
,
define
\varphi:\partial_{D}R_{l}\to[0,1]
as

\varphi(w_{1},w_{2}):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{1-w_{2}^{2}}&{\rm if}~(w_{1},w_{2})\in\{0\}\times[-1,1],\\
0&{\rm if}~(w_{1},w_{2})\in(0,l)\times\{-1\}.\end{cases}

(11.21)

Definition 11.10

(
The functional
{\mathcal{F}}_{l}
)
.

Given

h\in L^{\infty}([0,l],[-1,1])
and
\psi\in{\rm BV}(R_{l};[0,1])

we define

{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi):=\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{l})-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{l}}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(0,l)\times\{1\}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(11.22)

We further define

X_{l}:=\{(h,\psi):h\in L^{\infty}([0,l],[-1,1]),\psi\in{\rm BV}(R_{l},[0,1]),\psi=0~{\rm in~}R_{l}\setminus SG_{h}\}.

(11.23)

Remark 11.11
.

(i)

The Borel function
h_{k,\varepsilon}:[0,l]\rightarrow[-1,1]
satisfies
h_{k,\varepsilon}=1
in
[0,\varepsilon)
, and
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}\in BV([0,l]\times[-1,1])
is such that
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}=0

almost everywhere in
R_{l}\setminus SG_{h_{k,\varepsilon}}
. Moreover
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(w_{1},w_{2})=\sqrt{1-w_{2}^{2}}{\rm~for~}(w_{1},w_{2})\in(0,\varepsilon)\times[-1,1]
,
and
\psi_{k,\varepsilon}(\cdot,-1)=0~{\rm in~}[0,l]
.
In particular

(h_{k,\varepsilon},\psi_{k,\varepsilon})\in X_{l}.

(ii)

if
(h,\psi)\in X_{l}
,
and if
h
is smaller than
1
almost everywhere on
(0,l)

then
the last
addendum on the right-hand side of (
11.22
) vanishes.

(iii)

Thanks to (
11.18
) and (
11.19
),
it follows from Remark
11.9
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}\big((\partial^{*}SG_{\psi_{k,\varepsilon}})\cap(R_{l}\times[0,+\infty)\big)-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h_{k,\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi_{k,\varepsilon},\overline{R}_{l}\setminus\big(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\}\big))-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h_{k,\varepsilon}})={\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h_{k,\varepsilon},\psi_{k,\varepsilon}).

As a consequence, from Lemma
11.7
we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\widehat{\vartheta}_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(4)}|+|\mathcal{G}_{-\widehat{\vartheta}_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(4)}|~\geq~

\displaystyle 2{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h_{k,\varepsilon},\psi_{k,\varepsilon}).

(11.24)

Notice that in minimizing
{\mathcal{F}}_{l}
we have a free boundary condition on the edge
\{l\}\times[-1,1]
.
By Remark
11.11
(i) and (
11.24
)
we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\widehat{\vartheta}_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(4)}|+|\mathcal{G}_{-\widehat{\vartheta}_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(4)}|\geq 2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi),

(11.25)

which leads to the investigation of the minimum problem on the right-hand side.

Remark 11.12
.

Let
(h,\psi)\in X_{l}
.
If
t_{0}\in(0,l)
is a Lebesgue point for
h
, and if
h(t_{0})<1
, then the trace of
\psi
over the segment
\{w_{1}=t_{0},\;h(t_{0})\leq w_{2}\leq 1\}

vanishes. Indeed
for any
\eta>0
we can find
\delta_{\eta}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}|h(w_{1})-h(t_{0})|~dw_{1}<\eta\qquad\forall\delta\in(0,\delta_{\eta}).

(11.26)

Let now
s_{0}\in(-1,1)
be such that
h(t_{0})<s_{0}\leq 1
(i.e.,
(t_{0},s_{0})\in\{w_{1}=t_{0},\;w_{2}>h(t_{0})\}
), and set

2\Delta:=s_{0}-h(t_{0})
.
By
Chebyschev inequality and (
11.26
) it follows that the set
B_{\Delta}:=\{w_{1}\in(t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta):|h(w_{1})-h(t_{0})|>\Delta\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(B_{\Delta})\leq\frac{2\delta\eta}{\Delta}.

(11.27)

Then, for any
\xi\in(0,\Delta)
we
infer
55
55

55

In the first inequality we have used that
0\leq\psi\leq 1
;
in the second inequality
that
SG_{h}
is the subgraph of
h
in
(0,l)\times(-1,1)
;
in the third inequality we have used that
s_{0}-h(t_{0})=2\Delta
and that
\xi<\Delta
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}\int_{s_{0}-\xi}^{s_{0}+\xi}\psi(w_{1},w_{2})~dw_{2}dw_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}\int_{s_{0}-\xi}^{s_{0}+\xi}\chi_{\{\psi>0\}}(w_{1},w_{2})~dw_{2}dw_{1}

(11.28)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}\int_{s_{0}-\xi}^{s_{0}+\xi}\chi_{SG_{h}}(w_{1},w_{2})~dw_{2}dw_{1}\leq\frac{\xi}{\delta}\int_{t_{0}-\delta}^{t_{0}+\delta}\chi_{B_{\Delta}}(w_{1})dw_{1}\leq\frac{2\xi\eta}{\Delta},

where the penultimate inequality follows from the inclusions

\displaystyle SG_{h}\cap\big([t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta]\times[s_{0}-\xi,s_{0}+\xi]\big)

\displaystyle\subseteq SG_{h}\cap\big([t_{0}-\delta,t_{0}+\delta]\times[s_{0}-\Delta,s_{0}+\Delta]\big)

\displaystyle\subseteq B_{\Delta}\times[s_{0}-\Delta,s_{0}+\Delta],

and the last inequality follows from (
11.27
).
Now (
11.28
)
entails the claim by the arbitrariness of
\eta>0
and since
\psi\geq 0
.

We now refine the choice of the class of pairs
(h,\psi)
where the infimum in (

) is computed.

Definition 11.13

(
The classes
\mathcal{H}_{l}
and

X_{l}^{\rm conv}
)
.

We set

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{l}:=\{h\in L^{\infty}([0,l],[-1,1]):\;h\text{ convex and
nonincreasing in }[0,l],h(0)=1\},

\displaystyle X_{l}^{\rm conv}:=\{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}:~h\in\mathcal{H}_{l}\}.

Proposition 11.14

(
Convexifying
h
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)=\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

(11.29)

Proof.

It is enough to show
the inequality “
\geq
”.
By extending
\psi
outside
R_{l}
as

\psi:=0
in
((0,l)\times\mathbb{R})\setminus R_{l}
,
we see that

{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)=\mathbb{A}\left(\psi,\overline{R}_{l}\setminus\big(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\}\big)\right)-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h})+\int_{\{0\}\times[-1,1]}|\psi^{-}-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

(11.30)

where, with a little abuse of notation,

\mathbb{A}\left(\zeta,\overline{R}_{l}\setminus\big(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\}\big)\right)=\mathbb{A}(\zeta,{R}_{l})+\int_{(0,l)\times\{1,-1\}}|\zeta^{-}|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

\zeta^{-}
being the trace of
\zeta\in BV(R_{l})
on
(0,l)\times\{1,-1\}
.

The thesis of the proposition will follow from the next three observations:

(1)

If
h\in\mathcal{H}_{l}

is such that
h(t_{0})=-1
for some Lebesgue point
t_{0}\in(0,l)
,
then the subgraph
SG_{h}

of
h

splits in two mutually disjoint components:

SG_{h}^{-}=SG_{h}\cap\{w_{1}<t_{0}\}

and
SG_{h}^{+}=SG_{h}\cap\{w_{1}>t_{0}\}
.
Let
\psi\in BV(R_{l},[0,1])
be such that

\displaystyle\psi=0\qquad\text{~a.e.~in~ }R_{l}\setminus SG_{h}.

The trace of
\psi
over the segment
\{w_{1}=t_{0},\;h(t_{0})\leq w_{2}\leq 1\}
is
0
, as a consequence of Remark
11.12
.
Then the function
\psi^{\star}:R_{l}\to[0,1]
defined as

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\begin{cases}\psi(w_{1},w_{2})&\text{if }w_{1}<t_{0},\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

still satisfies
(h,\psi^{\star})\in X_{l}
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi^{\star})\leq{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

Being
\psi^{\star}
identically zero in
\{w_{1}>t_{0}\}
, in particular
in
SG_{h}\cap\{w_{1}>t_{0}\}
, we can introduce

\displaystyle h^{\star}(w_{1}):=\begin{cases}h(w_{1})&\text{if }w_{1}<t_{0},\\
-1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

so that
(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})\in X_{l}

and we easily see that

{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})\leq{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi^{\star})
;
hence

{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})\leq{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

(2)

More generally, let
(h,\psi)\in X_{l}
and let
t_{0}\in(0,l)

be any Lebesgue point of
h
; we can also suppose that
h(t_{0})<1
.
Consider

\displaystyle h^{\star}(w_{1}):=\begin{cases}h(w_{1})&\text{if }w_{1}<t_{0},\\
h(w_{1})\wedge h(t_{0})&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(11.31)

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\begin{cases}\psi(w_{1},w_{2})&\text{if }w_{1}<t_{0},\\
\psi(w_{1},w_{2})&\text{if }w_{1}\geq t_{0},\;w_{2}\leq h(t_{0}),\\
0&\text{otherwise. }\end{cases}

We
claim that
{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})\leq{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

Define

U:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\in(0,l)\times(-1,1):w_{1}>t_{0},\;h(t_{0})<w_{2}<h(w_{1})\},

that is the set where we have replaced
\psi
by
0
.
To prove the claim, using (
11.30
) and
the equalities

\int_{\{0\}\times[-1,1]}|\psi^{-}-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\int_{\{0\}\times[-1,1]}|{\psi^{\star}}^{-}-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h^{\star}})=\mathcal{H}^{2}(U\cup(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h}))=\mathcal{H}^{2}(U)+\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{l}\setminus SG_{h}),

we have to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}\left(\psi^{\star},\overline{R}_{l}\setminus\big(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\}\big)\right)\leq\mathbb{A}\left(\psi,\overline{R}_{l}\setminus\big(\{w_{1}=0\}\cup\{w_{1}=l\}\big)\right)+\mathcal{H}^{2}(U).

(11.32)

Assume that
U
is non-empty and that
\mathcal{H}^{2}(U)>0
.
It is convenient to introduce

V:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\in R_{l}:t_{0}<w_{1}<l,\;h(w_{1})\vee h(t_{0})\leq w_{2}<1\},

so that

U\cup V=\{(w_{1},w_{2}):w_{1}>t_{0},\;h(t_{0})<w_{2}<1\}
is an open rectangle.
Since we have modified
\psi
only in
U
, inequality
(
11.32
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi^{\star},{U\cup V})+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{h(t_{0})\}}|{\psi^{\star}}^{+}-{\psi^{\star}}^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{1\}}|{\psi^{\star}}^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}

(11.33)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,{U\cup V})+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{h(t_{0})\}}|\psi^{+}-\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{1\}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\mathcal{H}^{2}(U),

with

\psi^{\pm}
(resp.

{\psi^{\star}}^{\pm}
)
the external and internal traces of
\psi

(resp.
\psi^{\star}
) on
\partial(U\cup V)
;
here we have used from Remark
11.12

that the trace of
\psi
on
\{t_{0}\}\times(h(t_{0}),1)

is zero (hence
\int_{\{t_{0}\}\times(h(t_{0}),1)}|\psi^{+}-\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\int_{\{t_{0}\}\times(h(t_{0}),1)}|{\psi^{\star}}^{+}-{\psi^{\star}}^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}=0
)
and that the external traces
\psi^{+}
,
{\psi^{\star}}^{+}

on
(t_{0},l)\times\{1\}
vanish as well.
Hence, exploiting that
\psi^{\star}=0
on
U\cup V
, so that

\mathbb{A}(\psi^{\star},U\cup V)=\mathcal{H}^{2}(U)+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)
, and that
\psi^{\star}=\psi
on
R_{l}\setminus(U\cup V)
,
inequality (
11.33
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{h(t_{0})\}}|\psi^{+}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}

(11.34)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,{U\cup V})+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{h(t_{0})\}}|\psi^{+}-\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(t_{0},l)\times\{1\}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}.

We split

(t_{0},l)=H_{1}\cup H_{2}\cup H_{3},

with
H_{1}:=\{w_{1}\in(t_{0},l):h(w_{1})=1\}
,

H_{2}:=\{w_{1}\in(t_{0},l):h(t_{0})\leq h(w_{1})<1\}
, and

H_{3}:=\{w_{1}\in(t_{0},l):h(w_{1})<h(t_{0})\}
.
Since
\mathbb{A}(\psi;U\cup V)=\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\psi}\cap((U\cup V)\times\mathbb{R}))
, by slicing and looking at
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}
as an
integral current, we have
56
56

56

Here we use that
D_{w_{2}}\psi=0
in
V
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,U\cup V)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{(t_{0},l)}\mathcal{H}^{1}\Big((\mathcal{G}_{\psi})_{t}{\cap((t_{0},l)\times(h(t_{0}),1)\times\mathbb{R}})\Big)~dt

\displaystyle\geq\int_{(t_{0},l)}\int_{(h(t_{0}),1)}|D_{w_{2}}\psi(t,s)|~\;dt+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)

\displaystyle=\int_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}}\int_{(h(t_{0}),1)}|D_{w_{2}}\psi(t,s)|~\;dt+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{H_{2}}|\psi^{-}(t,h(t_{0}))|~dt+\int_{H_{1}}|\psi^{-}(t,h(t_{0}))-\psi^{-}(t,1)|~dt+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{H_{1}\cup H_{2}}|\psi^{-}(t,h(t_{0}))|~dt-\int_{H_{1}}|\psi^{-}(t,1)|~dt+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)

\displaystyle=\int_{(t_{0},l)}|\psi^{-}(t,h(t_{0}))|~dt-\int_{H_{1}}|\psi^{-}(t,1)|~dt+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V)

\displaystyle=\int_{(t_{0},l)}|\psi^{-}(t,h(t_{0}))|~dt-\int_{(t_{0},l)}|\psi^{-}(t,1)|~dt+\mathcal{H}^{2}(V),

where
(\mathcal{G}_{\psi})_{t}
is the slice of
\mathcal{G}_{\psi}

on the plane
\{w_{1}=t\}
,
that is the generalized graph of the function
\psi\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits\{w_{2}=t\}
.
From the above expression, the
triangular inequality
implies (
11.34
).

(3)

Let
(h,\psi)\in X_{l}
. Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in(\varepsilon,l)

be Lebesgue points for

with
t_{1}<t_{2}
,
and let

. We consider the following modifications of

and

:

\displaystyle h^{\#}(w_{1}):=\begin{cases}h(w_{1})&\text{if }0<w_{1}<t_{1}\text{ or }l>w_{1}>t_{2},\\
h(w_{1})\wedge r_{12}(w_{1})&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\psi^{\#}(w_{1},w_{2}):=\begin{cases}\psi(w_{1},w_{2})&\text{if }0<w_{1}<t_{1}\text{ or }l>w_{1}>t_{2},\\
\psi(w_{1},w_{2})&\text{if }w_{1}\in[t_{1},t_{2}]~\textrm{and}~w_{2}\leq r_{12}(w_{1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise. }\end{cases}

In other words we set
\psi
equal to
0
above the segment
L_{12}
connecting
(t_{1},h(t_{1}))
to
(t_{2},h(t_{2}))
.
Also in this case we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h^{\#},\psi^{\#})\leq{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

(11.35)

Indeed, if
h(t_{1})=h(t_{2})
the proof is
identical to the case (2). Otherwise, it can be obtained by slicing as well,
parametrizing
L_{12}
by an arc length parameter,
then slicing the region
\{(w_{1},w_{2}):w_{1}\in(t_{1},t_{2}),\;w_{2}\in(\ell_{12}(w_{1}),1)\}
57
57

57

\ell_{12}
represents the affine function whose graph is
L_{12}
.
by lines perpendicular to
L_{12}
, and
exploiting the fact that
\psi
equals zero on the segments
\{t_{i}\}\times(h(t_{i}),1)
.

Let
(h,\psi)\in X_{l}
be given;
from (3) we
can always replace
h
by its convex envelope
and modifying accordingly

\psi
, we get two functions

and
\psi^{\#}

such that (

) holds. Moreover, by (2),

so that

The assertion of the proposition follows.
∎

Let us rewrite the functional
{\mathcal{F}}_{l}
in a convenient way.
Let
(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}
, and let
G_{h}=\{(w_{1},h(w_{1})):w_{1}\in(0,l)\}\subset\overline{R}_{l}

be the graph of
h
. We have, using (
11.22
),

{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)=\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{G_{h}\setminus\{h=-1\}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{l}}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

(11.36)

where, in the integral over
G_{h}
,
we consider the trace of
\psi\mathop{\hbox{\vrule height=7.0pt,width=0.5pt,depth=0.0pt\vrule height=0.5pt,width=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}}\nolimits SG_{h}
on
G_{h}
.

Corollary 11.15
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{D_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geq 2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)-\pi\varepsilon-\frac{C}{\varepsilon n}-o_{k}(1),

(11.37)

for an absolute constant
C>0
, and where the sequence
o_{k}(1)

depends on
\varepsilon
and
n
and is infinitesimal as
k\to+\infty
.

Proof.

From (
11.25
) and Proposition
11.14
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\widehat{\vartheta}_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(4)}|+|\mathcal{G}_{-\widehat{\vartheta}_{k,\varepsilon}}^{(4)}|\geq 2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

(11.38)

Combining (
11.38
) with (
9.12
),
inequality (
11.37
) follows.
∎

11.1
Lower bound: reduction to a Plateau-type
problem on the rectangle
R_{l}

We now state and prove our first main result.

Theorem 11.16

(
Lower bound for the area of the vortex map
)
.

The relaxed area of the graph of the vortex map
u
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{A}}(u,\Omega)\geq\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx+2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

(11.39)

Proof.

We write

\mathcal{A}(u_{k},\Omega)=\mathcal{A}(u_{k},\Omega\setminus D_{k})+\mathcal{A}(u_{k},D_{k})=\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx+\int_{D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx.

Therefore

\overline{\mathcal{A}}(u,\Omega)\geq\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx+\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx.

(11.40)

Given
\varepsilon\in(0,l)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, from (
4.37
) it follows

\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\Omega\setminus D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx\geq\int_{\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx-\frac{1}{n}-\frac{2}{\varepsilon n}.

(11.41)

From (
11.37
) we have

\int_{D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx=|\text{{\rm\textlbrackdbl}}{G_{u_{k}}}\text{{\rm\textrbrackdbl}}|_{D_{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geq 2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)-\pi\varepsilon-\frac{C}{\varepsilon n}-o_{k}(1).

(11.42)

Exploiting the fact that the right-hand side of (
11.38
)
does not depend on
k
, we can pass to the liminf as
k\rightarrow+\infty
in the above expression, to obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{D_{k}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|~dx\geq 2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)-\pi\varepsilon-\frac{C}{\varepsilon n}.

(11.43)

From (
11.40
), (
11.41
)
and (
11.43
) we obtain

\overline{\mathcal{A}}(u,\Omega)\geq\int_{\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx+2\inf_{(\psi,h)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(\psi,h)-\pi\varepsilon-\frac{C+2}{\varepsilon n}-\frac{1}{n},

(11.44)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,l)
.
Letting
n\to+\infty

and then
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,
by the dominated convergence theorem
(since
\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\Omega
as

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
) we get

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{A}}(u,\Omega)

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Big(\int_{\Omega\setminus\overline{{\rm B}}_{\varepsilon}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx+2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)-\pi\varepsilon\Big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx+2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi).

∎

12
Structure of minimizers of
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}

In this section we analyse the minimum problem on the
right-hand side of (
11.29
).
We prove the existence of minimizers, and exploy it to show
that the inequality (
11.39
)
in Theorem
11.16
is optimal.
First it is convenient to write the analogue of
{\mathcal{F}}_{l}

in a doubled rectangle, see (
12.4
).

We start by introducing some notation. We denote
R_{2l}

the open doubled
rectangle,
R_{2l}:=(0,2l)\times(-1,1)
, and define its Dirichlet boundary
58
58

58

Note
that
\partial_{D}R_{2l}
consists of three edges of

\partial R_{2l}
,
while
\partial_{D}R_{l}
(see (
11.1
))
consists of two edges of
\partial R_{l}
.

\partial_{D}R_{2l}\subset\partial R_{2l}
as

\displaystyle\partial_{D}R_{2l}:=\big(\{0,2l\}\times[-1,1]\big)\cup\big((0,2l)\times\{-1\}\big),

so that

\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}=(0,2l)\times\{1\}
.

Definition 12.1
.

We set

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{2l}=\big\{h:[0,2l]\to[-1,1],~h{\rm~convex},~h({w_{1}})=h(2l-{w_{1}})~\forall{w_{1}}\in[0,2l]\big\}.\end{split}

(12.1)

For each
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
, we further define

G_{h}:=\{({w_{1}},h({w_{1}})):{w_{1}}\in(0,2l)\},\qquad SG_{h}:=\{({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in R_{2l}:{w_{2}}<h({w_{1}})\},

where
SG_{h}:=\mathchar 31\relax

in the case
h\equiv-1
.
We set

L_{h}:=\Big(\{0\}\times(h(0),1)\Big)\cup\Big(\{2l\}\times(h(2l),1)\Big),

(12.2)

which is either empty,
or the union of two equal intervals,
see Fig.
17
.

Figure 17:
the graph of a convex symmetric
function
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
;

L_{h}
, defined in (
12.2
),
consists of the two vertical segments
over the boundary of
(0,2l)
, from
h(0)=h(2l)
to
1
.

Define

\varphi:\partial_{D}R_{2l}\to[0,1],\qquad\varphi({w_{1}},{w_{2}}):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{1-{w_{2}}^{2}}&\text{ if }({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in\{0,2l\}\times[-1,1],\\
0&\text{ if }({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in(0,2l)\times\{-1\}.\end{cases}

(12.3)

The graph of
\varphi
on
\{0,2l\}\times[-1,1]

consists of two half-circles of radius
1

centered at
(0,0)
and
(2l,0)
respectively,
see Fig.
18
.
We notice that such a
\varphi

extends definition (
11.21
).

Figure 18:
the graph of the boundary condition function
\varphi

in (
12.3
) on the Dirichlet
boundary of
R_{2l}
. We also draw the graph of a function

h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
, and the
two segments
L_{h}
.

Definition 12.2

(
The functional
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
)
.

We define

X_{2l}^{\rm conv}:=\left\{(h,\psi):h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l},\psi\in BV(R_{2l},[0,1]),\psi=0\ {\rm on}~R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h}\right\},

and for any
(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
,

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi):=\mathbb{A}(\psi;R_{2l})-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi-\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(12.4)

Remark 12.3
.

(i)

The only case in which
the last addendum on the right-hand side of (
12.4
) may be positive
is when
h
is identically
1
on
\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}
;

(ii)

We have

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)=\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}SG_{h}}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{G_{h}\setminus\{w_{2}=-1\}}|\psi^{-}|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{L_{h}}\varphi~d\mathcal{H}^{1},

(12.5)

where

\partial_{D}SG_{h}:=(\partial_{D}R_{2l})\cap\partial SG_{h},

(12.6)

and
\psi^{-}
denotes the trace of
\psi
from the side of
SG_{h}
.
To show (
12.5
), we start to observe that,
using that
\psi=0
on
R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h}
, it follows

\int_{\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\int_{G_{h}\cap\{w_{2}=1\}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}
. This last term is nonzero only if
h\equiv 1
, in which case
L_{h}
is empty, and the equivalence between (
12.4
) and (
12.5
) easily follows. If instead
h
is not identically
1
, then, using again that
\psi=0

on
R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h}
, we see that the last term on
the right-hand side of (
12.4
) is null,
and

\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})=\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l})-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h})-\int_{G_{h}\cap R_{2l}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(12.7)

Hence, if
h
is not identically
1
,
inserting
(
12.7
) into (
12.4
),
we obtain, splitting
\partial_{D}R_{2l}=(\partial_{D}SG_{h})\cup L_{h}\cup(G_{h}\cap\{w_{2}=-1\})
, and using
that
\varphi=0
on
(0,2l)\times\{-1\}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l})-\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi-\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi-\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{G_{h}\cap R_{2l}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}SG_{h}}|\psi-\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{L_{h}}|\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{G_{h}\cap\{w_{2}=-1\}}|\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{G_{h}\cap R_{2l}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}SG_{h}}|\psi-\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{G_{h}\setminus\{w_{2}=-1\}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{L_{h}}\varphi d\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(iii)

We have

\displaystyle\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)

(12.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\big\{\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}SG_{h}}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{G_{h}\setminus\{w_{2}=-1\}}|\psi^{-}|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{L_{h}}\varphi~d\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle:h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}\setminus\{h\equiv-1\},~\psi\in{\rm BV}(SG_{h},[0,1])\big\}.

(iv)

If
h>-1
everywhere, then
SG_{h}
is connected,

\partial_{D}SG_{h}=\partial_{D}R_{2l}\setminus L_{h}
,
and the sum of the first three terms on
the right-hand side of (
12.5
) gives the area
of the graph of
\psi

on
\overline{SG_{h}}
, with the boundary condition
\varphi
set to be
0
on
G_{h}
.

(v)

Our aim is to have a surface in

\overline{R}_{2l}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{2}_{({w_{1}},{w_{2}})}\times\mathbb{R}
, of graph type, whose boundary consists of the union of the graph of
\varphi
and
the graph of a convex function
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
. The last three terms in (
12.5
) are an area penalization to force the solution to attain these boundary conditions by filling, with vertical walls, the gap between the boundary of any
competitor surface (the generalized graph of
\psi
) and the required boundary conditions. In particular the presence of the last term of (
12.5
) is explained as follows:
assume that
h(0)<1
,
i.e.
,
L_{h}\neq\mathchar 31\relax
; the graph of
any
\psi\in{\rm BV}(SG_{h},[0,1])
does not
reach the graph of
\varphi|_{L_{h}}
(simply because
L_{h}\cap\overline{SG_{h}}=\mathchar 31\relax
). To overcome this,
the graph of
\psi
is glued to the wall
consisting of the subgraph of

\varphi|_{L_{h}}
(inside
\overline{R}_{2l}
).

(vi)

Take
{h_{n}}:=-1+\frac{1}{n}
, and
\psi_{n}:=c>0
on
SG_{h_{n}}
,
then
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{A}(\psi_{n},SG_{h_{n}})=0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\partial_{D}SG_{h_{n}}}|\psi_{n}-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=2cl
, and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{G_{h_{n}}\setminus\{h_{n}=-1\}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=2cl
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{L_{h_{n}}}\varphi~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\pi
,
hence

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(-1,0)=\pi<\lim_{n\to+\infty}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h_{n},\psi_{n})=4cl+\pi,

that is the functional
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
in some sense forces a minimizing sequence to attain the boundary conditions as much as possible.

By symmetry, we easily infer

\displaystyle 2\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi)=\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi),

(12.9)

therefore
we can now restate the content of Theorem
11.16
as follows:

\overline{\mathcal{A}}(u,\Omega)\geq\int_{\Omega}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx+\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi).

(12.10)

Remark 12.4

(
Two explicit estimates from above
)
.

Let
h\equiv 1
and

\psi({w_{1}},{w_{2}}):=\sqrt{1-{w_{2}}^{2}}=:\psi_{s}(w_{1},w_{2})
for any
(w_{1},w_{2})\in R_{2l}
.
Then
(h,\psi)
is one of the competitors in (
12.9
) and
therefore

\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)\leq{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(1,\psi_{s})=2\pi l\qquad\forall l>0,

which is the lateral area of the cylinder
(0,2l)\times B_{1}
.
Also,
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
is well defined for
h\equiv-1
,
in which case
SG_{h}=\mathchar 31\relax
,
\psi\equiv 0
in
R_{2l}
,
and therefore

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(-1,0)=\int_{\{0,2l\}\times(-1,1)}\varphi~d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\pi,

(12.11)

which is the area of the two
half-disks joined by the segment
(0,2l)\times\{-1\}
,
see Fig.
18
. In particular

\inf_{(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}}\mathcal{F}(h,\psi)\leq\pi\qquad\forall l>0.

(12.12)

In the next section we shall
prove the existence and regularity of minimizers for the minimum
problem on the right-hand side of (
12.10
).

12.1
Existence of a minimizer of
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}

The construction of a suitable recovery
sequence for the relaxed area of the graph of the vortex map

u
depends on the existence and regularity
of one-codimensional area minimizing surfaces of graph type for problem
(
12.9
).
We start by analysing the features of the space

\mathcal{H}_{2l}
in (
12.1
).
Clearly the graph of
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
is symmetric with
respect to
\{{w_{1}}=l\}
; also, the convexity of
h
implies
h\in{\rm Lip}_{{\rm loc}}((0,2l))
,
and
h
has a
continuous extension on
[0,2l]
.

Lemma 12.5

(
Compactness of
\mathcal{H}_{2l}
)
.

Every sequence
(h_{k})\subset\mathcal{H}_{2l}
has a subsequence
converging uniformly on compact subsets of
(0,2l)

to some element of
\mathcal{H}_{2l}
.

Proof.

See for instance
[
22
, Sec. 1.1]
.
∎

It is convenient to extend
\varphi
in the doubled rectangle

\overline{R}_{2l}
by defining the extension
\widehat{\varphi}

as:

\widehat{\varphi}(w_{1},w_{2})=\widehat{\varphi}(0,w_{2}):=\sqrt{1-w_{2}^{2}}\qquad\forall(w_{1},w_{2})\in\overline{R}_{2l}.

(12.13)

In the rest of this section we want to prove the following result.

Theorem 12.6

(
Minimizing pairs
)
.

There exists
({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
such that

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})=\min\big\{{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi):(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}\big\},

(12.14)

and
\psi^{\star}
is symmetric with respect to

\{{w_{1}}=l\}\cap R_{2l}
.
Moreover, if
{h^{\star}}
is not identically
-1
, then

(i)

{h^{\star}}(0)=1={h^{\star}}(2l)
, and

{h^{\star}}>-1
in
(0,2l)
;

(ii)

\psi^{\star}
is locally Lipschitz (hence analytic)
and strictly positive
in
SG_{{h^{\star}}}
;

(iii)

\psi^{\star}

is continuous up to the boundary of
SG_{{h^{\star}}}
,
and attains the boundary conditions, i.e., for
({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in\partial SG_{h^{*}}
,

\psi^{\star}({w_{1}},{w_{2}})=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if}\quad{w_{2}}=-1\text{ or }{w_{2}}={h^{\star}}({w_{1}}),\\
\sqrt{1-{w_{2}}^{2}}&\text{ if}\quad{w_{1}}=0\text{ or }{w_{1}}=2l,\end{cases}

(12.15)

hence

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})=\mathbb{A}(\psi^{\star},SG_{{h^{\star}}});

(12.16)

(iv)

we have

\psi^{\star}<\widehat{\varphi}{\rm~in}~R_{2l}.

(12.17)

The rest of this section is devoted
to prove this theorem; we start with some preparation.

Definition 12.7

(
Convergence in
X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
)
.

We say that a sequence
(({h_{n}},\psi_{n}))\subset X_{2l}^{\rm conv}

converges to
(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
, if

-

({h_{n}})
converges
to
h

uniformly on compact subsets of
(0,2l)
;

-

(\psi_{n})
converges
to
\psi
in
L^{1}(R_{2l})
.

Lemma 12.8

(
Closedness of
X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
)
.

Let
(({h_{n}},\psi_{n}))\subset X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
be a sequence such that

({h_{n}})
converges
to
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}

uniformly on compact subsets of
(0,2l)
,
and
(\psi_{n})
converges
to
\psi\in{\rm BV}(R_{2l})

in
L^{1}(R_{2l})
.
Then
(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
.

Proof.

Possibly passing to a (not relabelled)
subsequence, we can assume that

(\psi_{n})

converges to
\psi
pointwise in
A\subseteq R_{2l}
, with

\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{2l}\setminus A)=0
, and

\psi_{n}=0
in
A\cap(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h_{n}})

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We only have to show that

\psi=0
in
A\cap(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h})
. We can also assume
that
A
does not intersect the graph of
h
.
If
({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in A\cap(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h})
,
then
{w_{2}}>h({w_{1}})
. From
the local uniform convergence of
({h_{n}})
to
h

in
(0,2l)
it follows that

{w_{2}}>{h_{n}}({w_{1}})
for
n
large enough,
i.e.,

({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in A\cap(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h_{n}})
,
and the assertion follows.
∎

Lemma 12.9

(
Lower semicontinuity of
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
)
.

Let
(({h_{n}},\psi_{n}))\subset X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
be a sequence
converging to
(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
in the sense of Definition
12.7
. Then

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)\leq\liminf_{n\to+\infty}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}({h_{n}},\psi_{n}).

(12.18)

Proof.

It is standard
59
59

59

Indeed, let
\tilde{\varphi}:\partial R_{2l}\to[0,1]
be defined as

\tilde{\varphi}:=\varphi
on
\partial_{D}R_{2l}
, and

\tilde{\varphi}:=0
on
\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}
.
Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be an open disc containing
\overline{R_{2l}}
.
We extend
\tilde{\varphi}
to a
W^{1,1}
function
in
B\setminus\overline{R_{2l}}
,

[
19
, Thm. 2.16]
,
and we still denote by
\tilde{\varphi}
such an extension.
For every
\psi\in BV(R_{2l})
, define

\widehat{\psi}:=\psi
in
R_{2l}
and

\widehat{\psi}:=\tilde{\varphi}
in
B\setminus R_{2l}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l})+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi-\varphi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l})+\int_{\partial R_{2l}}|\psi-\tilde{\varphi}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}=\mathbb{A}(\widehat{\psi},B)-\mathbb{A}(\tilde{\varphi},B\setminus\overline{R_{2l}}),

where the last equality follows from
[
19
, (2.15)]
.
Thus the lower semicontinuity of the functional in (
12.19
)
follows from the
L^{1}(B)
-lower semicontinuity of the area functional.

to show that
the functional

\psi\in BV(R_{2l},[0,1])\to\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l})+\int_{\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l}}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}

(12.19)

is
L^{1}(R_{2l})
-lower semicontinuous.
Since
({h_{n}})
converges to
h
pointwise in
(0,2l)
, we also have

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h_{n}})=\mathcal{H}^{2}(R_{2l}\setminus SG_{h})
.
The assertion follows.
∎

Proposition 12.10

(
Existence of a minimizer of (
12.14
)
)
.

There exists
({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
satisfying (
12.14
).

Proof.

Note that

h({w_{1}}):=-1,\quad\psi({w_{1}},{w_{2}}):=0,\qquad({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in R_{2l},

is a competitor in (
12.14
). Hence,
for a minimizing sequence
(({h_{n}},\psi_{n}))\subset X_{2l}^{\rm conv}
,
recalling (
12.11
)
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}({h_{n}},\psi_{n})=\inf\big\{{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi):(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm conv}\big\}\leq\pi.

(12.20)

Thus

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|D\psi_{n}|(R_{2l})<+\infty
, and
there exists
\psi^{\star}\in BV(R_{2l},[0,1])
such that, up to a
(not relabelled) subsequence,
(\psi_{n})

converges to
\psi^{\star}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
.

Using Lemmas
12.5
and

12.8
, we may assume that
(h_{n})

converges locally uniformly to some
{h^{\star}}\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
,
and
\psi^{\star}=0
in
R_{2l}\setminus SG_{{h^{\star}}}
.
The assertion then follows from
Lemma
12.9
.
∎

We now turn to the regularity of minimizers.

Proposition 12.11

(
Analyticity and positivity of a minimizer
)
.

Suppose that
(h,\psi)
is a
minimizer of (
12.14
), and that
h
is not identically
-1
.
Then
\psi
is analytic
in
SG_{h}
, and solves the equation

\displaystyle{\rm div}\Big(\frac{\nabla\psi}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi|^{2}}}\Big)=0\qquad{\rm in}~SG_{h}.

(12.21)

Moreover
\psi>0
in
SG_{h}
.

Proof.

Since by assumption
h
is not identically
-1
,
we have that
SG_{h}
is nonempty. Moreover minimality ensures

\int_{SG_{h}}\sqrt{1+|D\psi|^{2}}\leq\int_{SG_{h}}\sqrt{1+|D\psi_{1}|^{2}}

for any
\psi_{1}\in BV(SG_{h})
,
{\rm spt}(\psi-\psi_{1})\subset\subset SG_{h}
.
Thus,
by
[
19
, Thm
14.13]
,
\psi
is locally Lipschitz, and hence analytic, in

SG_{h}
, and
(
12.21
) follows.
Now, let
z\in SG_{h}
and take a
disc
B_{\eta}(z)\subset\subset SG_{h}
. Since

\psi\geq 0
on
\partial B_{\eta}(z)
we find, by
the strong maximum principle
[
19
, Thm. C.4]
, that
either
\psi
is identically zero in
B_{\eta}(z)
, or

\psi>0
in

B_{\eta}(z)
. Hence from the analyticity of
\psi
and the
arbitrariness of

z
, we have that either
\psi
is identically
zero in
SG_{h}
or
\psi>0
in
SG_{h}
.
Now

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,0)=|SG_{h}|+\pi>{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(-1,0)=\pi
, see
(
12.11
). Thus
(h,0)
is not a minimizer, and the positivity of
\psi
in
SG_{h}
is achieved.

∎

Lemma 12.12

(
Symmetric minimizers
)
.

There exists a minimizer
(h,\psi)
of (
12.14
)
such that

h(\cdot)=h(2l-\cdot)
and
\psi
is
symmetric with respect to
\{{w_{1}}=l\}\cap R_{2l}
.

Proof.

Let
(h,\psi)
be a minimizer of (
12.14
).
Let
I\subset(0,2l)
be an open interval; consistently
with (
12.4
), and since
\psi
is continuous in
SG_{h}
, we set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi;I):=

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi,I\times(-1,1))-\mathcal{H}^{2}\Big(I\times(-1,1)\setminus SG_{h}\Big)+\int_{(\partial_{D}R_{2l})\cap(\overline{I}\times[-1,1))}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{(\partial R_{2l}\setminus\partial_{D}R_{2l})\cap(\overline{I}\times(-1,1])}|\psi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}.

Recall that
h\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
, hence its graph
is symmetric with respect to
\{{w_{1}}=l\}\cap R_{2l}
.
Define

\tilde{\psi}:=\psi
on
(0,l)\times(-1,1)

and
\tilde{\psi}({w_{1}},{w_{2}}):=\psi(2l-{w_{1}},{w_{2}})

for
({w_{1}},{w_{2}})\in(l,2l)\times(-1,1)
, in particular
the graph of

\tilde{\psi}
is symmetric with
respect to
\{{w_{1}}=l\}\cap R_{2l}
.
Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi;(0,l))={\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi;(l,2l))
, it follows

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\tilde{\psi})={\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)

for, if
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi;(0,l))<{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi;(l,2l))
, then

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\tilde{\psi})<{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)
which contradicts the
minimality of
(h,\psi)
.
∎

Lemma 12.13
.

Suppose that
(h,\psi)
is a
minimizer of (
12.14
) such
that:

(i)

h(\cdot)=h(2l-\cdot)
;

(ii)

\psi
is
symmetric with respect to
\{{w_{1}}=l\}\cap R_{2l}
;

(iii)

h
is not identically
-1
.

Then

h({w_{1}})>-1\qquad\forall{w_{1}}\in[0,2l].

Proof.

By the symmetry of
h
and
\psi
with respect to

\{{w_{1}}=l\}\cap R_{2l}

(Lemma
12.12
) we may restrict our argument to
[0,l]
.
Assume by contradiction that there exists
\overline{w}_{1}\in(0,l]
such that
h(\overline{w}_{1})=-1
.
Recall that
h
is convex,
nonincreasing in
[0,l]
and continuous at
l
.
Let

w_{1}^{0}:=\min\{{w_{1}}\in(0,l]:h({w_{1}})=-1\}.

Since
h
is not identically
-1
,
we have
w_{1}^{0}>0
, and
h
is strictly decreasing in
(0,w_{1}^{0})
. Let

h^{-1}:[-1,h(0)]\rightarrow[0,w_{1}^{0}]

be the inverse of
h|_{[0,w_{1}^{0}]}
.
We have,
using (
12.5
) and (
12.6
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{\partial_{D}SG_{h}}|\psi-\varphi|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{G_{h}\setminus\{w_{2}=-1\}}|\psi^{-}|~d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{L_{h}}\varphi~d\mathcal{H}^{1}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}(\psi,SG_{h})+\int_{(-1,h(0))}|\psi(0,{w_{2}})-\varphi(0,{w_{2}})|~d{w_{2}}+\int_{(0,w_{1}^{0})}|\psi({w_{1}},-1)-\varphi({w_{1}},-1)|~d{w_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\ \ \qquad+\int_{G_{h{\llcorner{(0,w_{1}^{0})}}}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(h(0),1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}.

Now,
we argue by slicing
the rectangle
R_{l}=(0,l)\times(-1,1)

with lines
\{{w_{1}}=\tau\},\tau\in(0,l)
.
Recalling the expression of
SG_{h}
(which is non empty
by assumption (iii)), and neglecting the third addendum,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{w_{1}^{0}}\int_{-1}^{h({w_{1}})}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi|^{2}}~d{w_{2}}d{w_{1}}

\displaystyle+\int_{(-1,h(0))}|\psi(0,{w_{2}})-\varphi(0,{w_{2}})|d{w_{2}}+\int_{(0,w_{1}^{0})}|\psi({w_{1}},-1)-\varphi({w_{1}},-1)|~d{w_{1}}

\displaystyle+\int_{G_{h{\llcorner{(0,w_{1}^{0})}}}}|\psi|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(h(0),1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{w_{1}^{0}}\int_{-1}^{h({w_{1}})}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi|^{2}}d{w_{2}}d{w_{1}}+\int_{(-1,h(0))}|\psi(0,{w_{2}})-\varphi(0,{w_{2}})|d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle+\int_{G_{h{\llcorner{(0,w_{1}^{0})}}}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(h(0),1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{{w_{1}}_{0}}\int_{-1}^{h({w_{1}})}|D_{w_{1}}\psi({w_{1}},{w_{2}})|d{w_{2}}d{w_{1}}+\int_{(-1,h(0))}|\psi(0,{w_{2}})-\varphi(0,{w_{2}})|d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle+\int_{G_{h{\llcorner(0,w_{1}^{0})}}}|\psi^{-}|d\mathcal{H}^{1}+\int_{(h(0),1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}},

where
D_{w_{1}}
stands for the partial derivative with respect to
w_{1}
.
Neglecting
\sqrt{1+(\frac{d}{ds}h^{-1})^{2}}
in the third addendum on the
right-hand side,
we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)>

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{h(0)}\int_{0}^{h^{-1}({w_{2}})}|D_{w_{1}}\psi({w_{1}},{w_{2}})|~d{w_{1}}d{w_{2}}+\int_{(-1,h(0))}|\psi(0,{w_{2}})-\varphi(0,{w_{2}})|d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle+\int_{(-1,h(0))}\psi(h^{-1}({w_{2}}),{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}+\int_{(h(0),1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{h(0)}\Big|\int_{0}^{h^{-1}({w_{2}})}D_{w_{1}}\psi({w_{1}},{w_{2}})d{w_{1}}\Big|d{w_{2}}-\int_{(-1,h(0))}\psi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle+\int_{(-1,h(0))}\psi(h^{-1}({w_{2}}),{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}+\int_{(-1,1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{(-1,h(0))}\lvert\psi(h^{-1}({w_{2}}),{w_{2}})-\psi(0,{w_{2}})\rvert d{w_{2}}-\int_{(-1,h(0))}\psi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle+\int_{(-1,h(0))}\psi(h^{-1}({w_{2}}),{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}+\int_{(-1,1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{(-1,1)}\varphi(0,{w_{2}})d{w_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(-1,0).

Hence the value of
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
on
the pair
h\equiv-1
,
\psi\equiv 0

is smaller than
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)
, thus
contradicting the minimality of
(h,\psi)
.
∎

We now prove point (iii) of Theorem
12.6
:
this will be a consequence of

12.16
.

Lemma 12.14
.

Let

Then
\psi

.

Proof.

The result follows from

since

∎

Remark 12.15

In the hypotheses of Lemma

Theorem 12.16

(12.24)

Step 2: Comparison with a Plateau problem.

and then contract it continuously to the middle point of
\{w_{2}=\widehat{s}\}\cap K_{n}
, showing that
\gamma
is homotopic to the constant curve.
Hence,
by the Riemann mapping theorem,
S_{h_{n}}
is biholomorphic to the
open unit disc, and

\overline{S}_{h_{h}}

is homeomorphic to the closure of the disc, thanks to the fact that
\partial S_{h_{n}}
is a Jordan curve, due to the BV-regularity of
h_{n}
.

.
Denoting by
\mathcal{G}_{\psi_{n}}^{+}
the graph of
\psi_{n}
over
S_{h_{n}}
,
we consider the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\psi_{n}}^{-}
of
-\psi_{n}
over
S_{h_{n}}
,
and observe that the closure of

\mathcal{G}_{\psi_{n}}^{+}\cup\mathcal{G}_{\psi_{n}}^{-}
is a disc-type surface with boundary
\Gamma_{n}
. Therefore,
by minimality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi_{n},S_{h_{n}})=\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{\psi_{n}}^{+})\geq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{n}^{+}).

(12.39)

Step 3: Passing to the limit as
n\rightarrow+\infty
: the surface
\Sigma
.

The graph of
\psi
over the segment
[0,2l]\times\{\bar{s}\}

and the graph of
\varphi
over the two segments

\{0,2l\}\times[\bar{s},1]
form a simple continuous curve
\Gamma^{+}
which,
excluding the two endpoints
P=(0,1,0)
and
Q=(2l,1,0)
,
is contained in the half-space
\{w_{3}>0\}
, while

P,Q\in\{w_{3}=0\}
(see
Fig.
19
).
If we consider

\Gamma:=\Gamma^{+}\cup\Gamma^{-},

with
\Gamma^{-}
the symmetric of
\Gamma^{+}
with respect to
\{w_{3}=0\}
, a direct check shows that the curves

\Gamma_{n}
converge to the curve
\Gamma

in the sense of
Frechet
[
27
]
, as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.
As a consequence, the area-minimizing disc-type surfaces
\Sigma_{n}
satisfy
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma)
(see
[
27
, Paragraphs 301, 305]
), with

\Sigma
a disc-type area-minimizing surface
spanned by
\Gamma
. It follows

\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{n}^{+})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma^{+})\qquad{\rm as}~n\to+\infty,

(12.40)

where
\Sigma^{+}:=\Sigma\cap\{w_{3}>0\}
.
From (
12.40
),
(
12.39
), (
12.38
)
we deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma^{+})=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma_{n}^{+})\leq\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{A}(\psi_{n},S_{h_{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left(\mathbb{A}(\psi_{n},K_{n})-\mathcal{H}^{2}(K_{n}\setminus S_{h_{n}})\right)=\mathcal{F}_{2l}(h,\psi)-\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l}\setminus K).

Since
\psi_{n}=\psi
on
R_{2l}\setminus K
, we
get

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left(\mathbb{A}(\psi_{n},S_{h_{n}})+\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l}\setminus K)\right)={\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)\geq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma^{+})+\mathbb{A}(\psi,R_{2l}\setminus K).

(12.41)

Let
\Phi=(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3}):\overline{B}_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a parametrization of
\Sigma
, which is
analytic and conformal in the open unit disc

B_{1}
and continuous up to

\partial B_{1}
with
\Phi(\partial B_{1})=\Gamma
.
Exploiting the results in
[
25
]
(see also
[
14
, pag. 343]
)
we know that

\Phi{\rm~is~an~embedding},

(12.42)

since
\Gamma
is a simple curve on
the boundary of the convex set
K\times\mathbb{R}
.

Now, we need to prove several qualitative properties of
\Sigma
.

Step 4:

\Sigma\cap\{w_{3}=0\}
is a simple curve
connecting
P
and
Q
.

This can be seen as follows:
Assume
\Phi(p_{0})=P
and
\Phi(q_{0})=Q
for two distinct points

.
By standard arguments
68
68

68

See also step 5 where a similar statement is proved.
,
the disc
B_{1}
is splitted into two
connected components
\{x\in B_{1}:\Phi_{3}(x)\geq 0\}

By the injectivity of

)) we conclude that

(12.43)

is a simple curve connecting

and more specifically

.

In the next step we show that, due to the particular

\Gamma

]

,
\Gamma_{0}

The projection

of

on

Take a point

In particular, the continuous function

From Rado’s lemma

69

69

.

If

and are points in

.

Before passing to step 6, recall the definition of
\Gamma_{0}
in
(

), and observe

.
Let us denote by

with boundary

.

We are now in a position to show that

admits

.

Step 6: Graphicality of
\Sigma^{+}
: the disc-type surface
\Sigma^{+}

can be written as a graph over the plane
\{w_{3}=0\}

of a
W^{1,1}
function

\widetilde{\psi}:U\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
.
At first we observe that if
\Sigma^{+}
is not Cartesian with
respect to
\{w_{3}=0\}
, then there is
some point
\mathcal{P}\in\Sigma^{+}\setminus\partial\Sigma^{+}

where the tangent plane to
\Sigma^{+}
is vertical
72
72

72

This can be
seen as follows: as shown in step 5,
the intersection between
\Sigma^{+}
and any plane
\{w_{1}=\textrm{cost}\}
,

{\rm cost}\in(0,2l)
, is a
simple curve with endpoints in
\partial\Sigma^{+}
. If
\Sigma^{+}
is not Cartesian, one of these curves
\gamma

is not Cartesian, and then there is a point where
the tangent vector to
\gamma
is vertical.
At such a point the tangent plane to
\Sigma^{+}

is vertical.

,
that is, it contains the
line
\{\mathcal{P}+(0,0,w_{3}):\;w_{3}\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
We will show,
with an argument similar to
the one needed to prove Rado’s Lemma
[
14
, Lemma 2, pag. 295]
,
that any vertical plane is tangent to
\Sigma

in
at most one point.

Claim:

If
\Pi
is a vertical plane which is tangent to
\Sigma
, then there is at most one point where

\Pi
and
\Sigma
are tangent.

Assume
\Pi
intersects the relative interior of
\Sigma
. It is easy to
see that the intersection between
\Pi
and the Jordan curve
\Gamma
consists at most
of four points
73
73

73

A vertical plane intersects
K
on a straight segment. In turn, this segment intersects
\partial K
in two points. If a vertical plane intersects
\Gamma
in a point
(W_{1},W_{2},W_{3})
, then
(W_{1},W_{2},0)\in\partial K
. Moreover this plane intersects
\Gamma
also at
(W_{1},W_{2},-W_{3})
. Thus, the points of intersection are at most four. The degenerate cases in which
\Pi
contains a full
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measured part of
\Gamma
are excluded by this analysis, because in these cases
\Pi
does not intersect the interior of
\Sigma
. Instead, the cases in which the intersection consists of
2
or
3
points are easier to treat, and we detail only the
4
-points case (notice that by the geometry of
\Gamma
, the case of
3
points occurs when this plane is tangent to
\Gamma
at one of the points
(0,1,0)
or
(2l,1,0)
).

p_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4
.
Let
f
be a linear function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
vanishing on
\Pi
.
Then

f\circ\Phi
is harmonic in
B_{1}

and continuous in
\overline{B}_{1}
; in addition, it vanishes
at
\{p_{i},\;i=1,2,3,4\}
, and alternates its sign on the four arcs

\overline{p_{i}p_{i+1}}

on
\partial B_{1}

with endpoints
p_{i}
.
With no loss of generality, we may assume
f\circ\Phi>0
on
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
and
\overline{p_{3}p_{4}}
.
By harmonicity of
f\circ\Phi
,
any connected component of the region
\{x\in\overline{B}_{1}:f\circ\Phi(x)>0\}
must contain part of
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
or
\overline{p_{3}p_{4}}
, so that we deduce that these connected components are at most two.

Assume now by contradiction that there are two distinct
points
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
of
\Sigma
such that
\Pi

is tangent to
\Sigma
at
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
.
Since
f\circ\Phi
has null differential at
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})

and
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
, the set
\{f\circ\Phi=0\}
,
in a neighbourhood of
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
, consists of
2m_{p}
analytic
curves crossing at
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
, whereas in a neighbourhood of

\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
, it consists of
2m_{q}
analytic curves crossing at

\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
. Therefore, in a neighbourhood of
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
,
the set
\{f\circ\Phi>0\}
counts at least
2
open regions
(and similarly at
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
). Let us call the two of
these regions
A_{i}
,
i=1,2
and
B_{i}
,
i=1,2
(
A_{i}
’s
around
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
and
B_{i}
’s around
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
).
By harmonicity each
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
must be connected to one of the
arcs
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
or
\overline{p_{3}p_{4}}
.
Hence some of these regions must belong to the same connected component
of
\{f\circ\Phi>0\}
. Then we are reduced to two following
cases (see Fig. (
20
)):

(Case A)

A_{1}
and
A_{2}
belong to the same connected
component
(say the one containing
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
). Hence we can construct two disjoint curves in
\{f\circ\Phi>0\}
, both joining
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
to a point in
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
, emanating from
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
, one in
region
A_{1}
and one in region
A_{2}
. This contradicts the
maximum principle, because these two curves would enclose a region
where
f\circ\Phi
takes also negative values, whereas its boundary is in
\{f\circ\Phi>0\}
.

(Case B)

A_{1}
and
B_{1}
are joined
to
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
and
A_{2}
and
B_{2}
are joined to
\overline{p_{3}p_{4}}
.
In this case we can construct four curves in
\{f\circ\Phi>0\}
:
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
emanating from
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{P})
in regions
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
and reaching
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
and
\overline{p_{3}p_{4}}
, respectively;
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
emanating from
\Phi^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
in regions
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
and reaching
\overline{p_{1}p_{2}}
and
\overline{p_{3}p_{4}}
, respectively.
The region enclosed between these
4
curves has boundary contained in
\{f\circ\Phi>0\}
and necessarily inside it
the function
f\circ\Phi
takes also negative values, again in contrast with the
maximum principle.

From the above discussion our claim follows.

We are now ready to conclude the proof of step 6:
suppose by contradiction that
\Sigma^{+}
is not Cartesian
with respect to
\{w_{3}=0\}
, and take a point
P^{+}\in\Sigma^{+}\setminus\Gamma

where the tangent plane
\Pi
to
\Sigma^{+}
at
P^{+}
is vertical. By symmetry of
\Sigma
, the point
P^{-}
,
defined as the symmetric of
P^{+}
with respect to the
rectangle
R_{2l}
, belongs to
\Sigma^{-}
, and the tangent plane to
\Sigma^{-}
at
P^{-}
is the same plane
\Pi
. This contradicts the claim.
We eventually observe that
\widetilde{\psi}
is analytic on the subgraph of
\widetilde{h}
, since its graph is
\Sigma^{+}
. We conclude that
\widetilde{\psi}
belongs to
W^{1,1}(SG_{\widetilde{h}})
, since also
its total variation is bounded by the area of its graph, which is finite.

On the left it is represented

12.16
. The point

Step 7: the pair

(\widetilde{h},\widetilde{\psi})
is an admissible competitor for
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
.

To see this, we recall that in step 5 we
proved that
\widetilde{h}
is convex and

\widetilde{h}(\cdot)=\widetilde{h}(2l-\cdot)
,
i.e.

\widetilde{h}\in\mathcal{H}_{2l}
.
Furthermore
\Sigma^{+}
is the graph of
\widetilde{\psi}
, and its projection on the plane
\{w_{3}=0\}
is the subgraph of
\widetilde{h}
. It follows that the area of the graph of
\widetilde{\psi}
is exactly the area of
\Sigma^{+}
upon
SG_{\widetilde{h}}
. Let us also recall the

W^{1,1}
regularity of
\widetilde{\psi}
proved in
step 6.
Setting
\widetilde{\psi}:=\psi
in
R\setminus K
we infer the
admissibility of
(\widetilde{h},\widetilde{\psi})
.

Step 8: Conclusion.
From (
12.41
) we deduce

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h,\psi)\geq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Sigma^{+})+\mathbb{A}(\psi,R\setminus K)={\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(\widetilde{h},\widetilde{\psi}),

(12.44)

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\widetilde{\psi}

is continuous on
\partial_{D}R_{2l}
. Hence, also
(\widetilde{h},\widetilde{\psi})
is a minimizer for
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
.
We
now show that
\widetilde{\psi}
is continuous and equals
0
on
G_{\widetilde{h}}
. Indeed
\Sigma=\Sigma^{+}\cup\Sigma^{-}
is
analytic, hence also
\widetilde{h}
is smooth (and convex).
Moreover we know that
\widetilde{\psi}
is smooth in

SG_{\widetilde{h}}
. If its trace
\widetilde{\psi}^{+}

on
G_{\widetilde{h}}
is strictly positive somewhere,
we infer that the vertical subset of
\Sigma^{+}
defined as

\{(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}):(w_{1},w_{2})\in G_{\widetilde{h}},\;w_{3}\in(0,\widetilde{\psi}^{+}(w_{1},w_{2}))\},

has positive
\mathcal{H}^{2}
-measure and
cannot have zero mean curvature (the only case in which
its mean curvature vanishes is when
\widetilde{h}
is linear,
but in this case
\Sigma^{+}
must be contained in a plane
containing
G_{\widetilde{h}}
which is impossible,
since
\Gamma^{+}
is not).
We conclude
\widetilde{\psi}^{+}=0
on
G_{\widetilde{h}}
.
Finally,
L_{\widetilde{h}}=\mathchar 31\relax
for, if
not, the vertical part of
\Sigma^{+}
obtained on
L_{\widetilde{h}}

is flat and then, by analyticity, also
\Sigma^{+}
is, a contradiction.
The thesis of the theorem, and hence of
Theorem
12.6
(iii), is achieved.
∎

To conclude the proof of
Theorem
12.6
,
it remains to show (iv).
The pair
(h\equiv 1,\widehat{\varphi})
, where
the function
\widehat{\varphi}
is as in (
12.13
),
is one of the competitors for
problem (
12.14
) (notice that
\widehat{\varphi}
attains the boundary condition); in addition,
its subgraph is strictly convex
(see Fig.
18
), hence
74
74

74

As observed,
the minimal surface
\Sigma^{+}
is the graph of
\psi^{\star}=\widetilde{\psi}
, and it must be contained in the convex envelope of
\Gamma
,
i.e.
, inside the subgraph of
\widehat{\varphi}
.
necessarily

\psi^{\star}\leq\widehat{\varphi}
in
\overline{R_{2l}}
(where we have taken
\psi^{\star}=\widetilde{\psi}
, the solution given by Theorem
12.16
).

Eventually, the strict inequality in (
12.17
)
is a consequence of the strong maximum principle: indeed, the internal points of a minimal surface are always strictly inside the convex hull of its boundary, with the only exception in the case of part of a plane
(see
[
27
, pag 63, section 70]
); so that internal points of
\Sigma^{+}
are strictly inside the the graph
G_{\widehat{\varphi}}
of
\widehat{\varphi}
(that is half of the lateral boundary of a cylinder).
∎

We conclude this section by observing a consequence of
Theorem
12.16
:
Let
G_{w}
be the graph in
R_{2l}
of a
function
w\in C([0,2l],(-1,1])
such that
w(0)=w(2l)=1
,
and consider the curve
\Gamma_{w}
obtained by concatenation
of
G_{w}
with the graph of
\varphi
over
\partial_{D}R_{2l}
.

Corollary 12.17
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2l}(h,\psi)=\inf\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{w}}(X_{{\rm min}}),

(12.45)

where
(h,\psi)\in X_{2l}^{\rm{conv}}

is a minimizer of
\mathcal{F}_{2l}
,
X_{{\rm min}}

is a parametrization of a disc-type
area-mininizing solution of
the Plateau problem spanning
\Gamma_{w}
(see (
2.9
)),
and the infimum is computed over all functions
w
as above.

The proof of this corollary
can be achieved by adapting the proof of Theorem
12.16
,
which shows that the solution to the
Plateau problem in (
12.45
) is Cartesian and the optimal
w
is convex.

13
Upper bound

A minimizer
(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})
of (
12.14
)
needs to be used
for constructing a recovery sequence
(u_{k})\subset{\rm Lip}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
see formulas (
13.25
) and (
13.27
):
we know that

\psi^{\star}

is locally Lipschitz, but not Lipschitz, in
R_{2l}
; therefore we need first a regularization
procedure.

Let
(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})
be a minimizer provided by
Theorem
12.6
, and assume that
h^{\star}
is not identically
-1
.
We fix an integer
m>0

and, recalling the definition of
\widehat{\varphi}
in
(
12.13
), define

\displaystyle\varphi_{m}:=\Big(\widehat{\varphi}-\frac{2}{m}\Big)\vee 0\qquad{\rm in}~\overline{R}_{2l}.

(13.1)

We observe that
\varphi_{m}
is Lipschitz continuous in
\overline{R}_{2l}
.
We then set

\displaystyle\psi^{\star}_{m}:=\Big(\big(\psi^{\star}-\frac{1}{m})\vee 0\big)\Big)\wedge\varphi_{m}\qquad{\rm in}~R_{2l}.

(13.2)

Since
\psi^{\star}
is locally Lipschitz in
R_{2l}
, an easy check
shows that
\psi^{\star}_{m}
is Lipschitz continuous in
R_{2l}
for
any
m
(with an unbounded Lipschitz constant as
m\rightarrow+\infty
).
This follows from the fact that

\psi^{\star}
is continuous up to the boundary of
R_{2l}

(see Theorem
12.6
(iii))
and hence

\psi^{\star}_{m}
coincides with
either
0
or
\varphi_{m}
in a
neighbourhood of
(\partial_{D}R_{2l})\cup G_{h^{\star}}
in
R_{2l}
. Furthermore still
\psi^{\star}_{m}=0
on the upper graph
\overline{R_{2l}}\setminus SG_{h^{\star}}=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\in\overline{R_{2l}}:w_{2}\geq h^{\star}(w_{1})\}
of
h^{\star}
.

Lemma 13.1

(
Properties of
\psi_{m}^{\star}
)
.

Let
(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})
be a minimizer of
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
as in
Theorem
12.6
and assume
h^{\star}
is not identically
-1
.
For all
m>0
let
\psi^{\star}_{m}
be defined as in (
13.2
).
Then:

(i)

\psi^{\star}_{m}
is
Lipschitz continuous in
\overline{SG_{h^{\star}}}
,
\psi^{\star}_{m}=0

on
([0,2l]\times\{-1\})\cup(\overline{R_{2l}}\setminus SG_{h^{\star}})
,
and
\psi^{\star}_{m}(0,\cdot)=\varphi_{m}(0,\cdot)
, so that
|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi^{\star}_{m}(0,\cdot)|\leq|\partial_{w_{2}}\varphi(0,\cdot)|=|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)|
a.e. in
[-1,1]
;

(ii)

(\psi^{\star}_{m})

converges to
\psi^{\star}
uniformly on
\{0,2l\}\times[-1,1]
as
m\to+\infty
;

(iii)

we have

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\mathbb{A}(\psi^{\star}_{m},SG_{h^{\star}})=\mathbb{A}(\psi^{\star},SG_{h^{\star}}).

(13.3)

As a consequence
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star}_{m})\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})
as
m\rightarrow+\infty
.

Proof.

(i) and (ii) are direct consequences of the definitions.
To show (iii)
we start to observe that

\psi^{\star}_{m}\rightarrow\psi^{\star}

pointwise in
R_{2l}
: indeed,
this follows from the definitions
of
\varphi^{\star}_{m}
and
\psi^{\star}_{m}
up to noticing that

\varphi_{m}\rightarrow\widehat{\varphi}
pointwise in
R_{2l}

as
m\rightarrow+\infty
,
and
\psi^{\star}\leq\widehat{\varphi}
on
R_{2l}
.
From Theorem
12.6
(iv) it follows that,
at any point
(w_{1},w_{2})\in R_{2l}
,
for
m
large enough

\varphi_{m}(w_{1},w_{2})>\psi^{\star}(w_{1},w_{2})

(since
\widehat{\varphi}(w_{1},w_{2})>\psi^{\star}(w_{1},w_{2})
),
so that

\psi^{\star}_{m}(w_{1},w_{2})=\psi^{\star}(w_{1},w_{2})-\frac{1}{m}
.
As a consequence the set
A_{m}:=\{0<\psi^{\star}-\frac{1}{m}<\varphi_{m}\}
satisfies

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\mathcal{H}^{2}(SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m})=0,

and on
A_{m}
it holds

\psi^{\star}_{m}=\psi^{\star}-\frac{1}{m}
and
\nabla\psi^{\star}_{m}=\nabla\psi^{\star}
. Moreover,
on
SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m}
,
either
\psi^{\star}_{m}=0
(and hence
\nabla\psi_{m}^{\star}=0
) or
\psi^{\star}_{m}=\varphi_{m}
(and hence
\nabla\psi^{\star}_{m}=\nabla\varphi_{m}
). Therefore

\int_{SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi^{\star}_{m}|^{2}}~dx\leq\int_{SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\varphi_{m}|^{2}}~dx

and

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\int_{SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi^{\star}_{m}|^{2}}~dx\leq\lim_{m\to+\infty}\int_{SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\varphi_{m}|^{2}}~dx=0,

because
|\nabla\varphi_{m}|
are
uniformly bounded in
L^{1}(R_{2l})
.
Also

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}(\psi^{\star}_{m},SG_{h^{\star}})=\int_{A_{m}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi^{\star}|^{2}}~dx+\int_{SG_{h^{\star}}\setminus A_{m}}\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi^{\star}_{m}|^{2}}~dx,

and (
13.3
) follows.
∎

The main result of this section reads
as follows.

Theorem 13.2

(
Upper bound for the area of the vortex map
)
.

The relaxed area of the graph of the vortex map
u
satisfies

\overline{\mathcal{A}}(u,{\Omega})\leq\int_{{\Omega}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|~dx+2\min\big\{{\mathcal{F}}_{l}(h,\psi):(h,\psi)\in X_{l}^{\rm conv}\big\}.

(13.4)

Proof of theorem
13.2
.

To prove the theorem, we need to construct a
sequence
({u_{k}})\subset\textrm{Lip}({\Omega},\mathbb{R}^{2})

converging to
u
in
L^{1}({\Omega},\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathcal{A}({u_{k}},{\Omega})\leq\int_{{\Omega}}|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u)|dx+{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star}),

where
({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})
is a pair
minimizing
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
as in Theorem
12.6
.
We can assume that
h^{\star}
is not identically

-1
, otherwise the result follows from
[
1
]
.

We will specify various subsets of
{\Omega}
and define the sequence

({u_{k}})
on each of these sets (see Fig.
21
). More precisely, we will define
u_{k}
as a map into
\mathbb{S}^{1}
in the largest sector (step 1). This construction is similar
to the one in
[
1
]
(see also Remark
13.3
below). The contribution of the area in this sector will equal, as
k\rightarrow\infty
, the first term in (
13.4
). The second term
will be instead provided by the contribution of
u_{k}
in region
C_{k}\setminus B_{r_{k}}
(step 2), where we will need the aid of
the functions
({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})
(suitably regularized, in order to render
u_{k}
Lipschitz continuous). The other regions surrounding
C_{k}\setminus B_{r_{k}}
are needed to glue
u_{k}
between the aforementioned regions. This is done in steps 3, 4 and 5,
where it is also proven that the corresponding
area contribution is negligible. Finally, in steps 6 and 7
we show the crucial estimates to prove (
13.4
).
In Fig.
21

this subdivion of the domain
\Omega
is drawn.

Remark 13.3
.

Our construction
differs from the one in
[
1
]
, even when in place of

({h^{\star}},\psi^{\star})
we use
(1,\sqrt{1-s^{2}})

(
i.e.
,
the one in Section
10.1
)
in the following sense.
We use the full
graph of
\pm\psi^{\star}
to construct

u_{k}
(and therefore, in the case
when

is replaced by
(1,\sqrt{1-s^{2}})
,
the image of
u_{k}
covers the whole cylinder and not
only a part of it). Since
h^{\star}

may be
not identically
1
(and actually is not explicit in general),
the presence of a new set
T_{k}

is now needed, as an intermediate region to glue the
trace of
u_{k}
along two segments
\{\theta=\pm\overline{\theta}_{k}\}
.
The image set
u_{k}(T_{k})
covers a small
part of the unit circle.
See Fig.
21
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
({r_{k}}),(\theta_{k}),(\overline{\theta}_{k})

be infinitesimal sequences of positive numbers
such that
\overline{\theta}_{k}-\theta_{k}=:\delta_{k}>0
.
We shall suppose
75
75

75

This assumption will be used only in step 7.

\lim_{k\to+\infty}(\theta_{k}k)=0.

(13.5)

Let
{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}
be the
open disc centered at the origin with radius
{r_{k}}
, and

{{C}_{k}}:=\{(r,\theta)\in[0,l)\times[0,2\pi):\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}]\cup[2\pi-\theta_{k},2\pi)\},

(13.6)

be the half-cone in

,
with vertex at the origin and aperture equal to

, see

21
. Let

T_{k}:=\{(r,\theta)\in[0,l)\times[0,2\pi):\theta\in[\theta_{k},\overline{\theta}_{k}]\cup[2\pi-\overline{\theta}_{k},2\pi-\theta_{k}]\}.

(13.7)

We set

C_{k}^{+}:=C_{k}\cap\{\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}]\},\quad C_{k}^{-}:=C_{k}\cap\{\theta\in[2\pi-\theta_{k},2\pi]\},

and
divide
{{C}_{k}}\cap({\Omega}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}})
into two sets

\displaystyle C_{k}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}:=\big(C_{k}^{+}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}\big)\cup\big(C_{k}^{-}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}\big).

(13.8)

Figure 21:
On the left the subdivision of
{\rm B}_{l}
in sectors.
Specifically, the sectors

.

Step 1.

Definition of
{u_{k}}
in
\overline{{\Omega}\setminus({{C}_{k}}\cup T_{k})}
.

In this step our
construction is similar to the one in
[
1
, Lem. 5.3]
,
see also (
10.1
); in order to define

,
in the source we use polar

(r,\theta)
and in the target Cartesian coordinates.
Define

{u_{k}}(r,\theta):=\begin{cases}u(r,\theta)=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),&(r,\theta)\in(\overline{{\Omega}\setminus({{C}_{k}}\cup T_{k})})\setminus{\rm B}_{r_{k}/2},\\
\Big(\cos(\frac{2r}{{r_{k}}}(\theta-\pi)+\pi),\sin(\frac{2r}{{r_{k}}}(\theta-\pi)+\pi)\Big),&(r,\theta)\in\overline{{\rm B}_{r_{k}/2}\setminus({{C}_{k}}\cup T_{k})}.\end{cases}

(13.9)

Observe that

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(0,0)=(-1,0)=u_{k}(r,\pi),\qquad r\in[0,l);

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(r,\overline{\theta}_{k})=(\cos\overline{\theta}_{k},\sin\overline{\theta}_{k}),\qquad{u_{k}}(r,2\pi-\overline{\theta}_{k})=(\cos\overline{\theta}_{k},\sin(-\overline{\theta}_{k})),\qquad r\in({r_{k}}/2,l);

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(r,\overline{\theta}_{k})=\Big(\cos(\frac{2r}{{r_{k}}}(\overline{\theta}_{k}-\pi)+\pi)~,~\sin(\frac{2r}{{r_{k}}}(\overline{\theta}_{k}-\pi)+\pi)\Big),\qquad r\in[0,{r_{k}}/2],

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(r,2\pi-\overline{\theta}_{k})=\Big(\cos(\frac{2r}{{r_{k}}}(\pi-\overline{\theta}_{k})+\pi)~,~\sin(\frac{2r}{{r_{k}}}(\pi-\overline{\theta}_{k})+\pi)\Big),\qquad r\in[0,{r_{k}}/2].

(13.10)

The relevant contribution to the area of the graph of
u_{k}
is the one
in region
C_{k}
, and more specifically in
C_{k}\setminus B_{r_{k}}
;
it is in this region that we need to use a minimizing pair of
{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}
.

Step 2.

Definition of
{u_{k}}
on
{{C}_{k}}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}
.

We first need a regularization of
h^{\star}
: assuming without
loss of generality
1/k<l
,
we define

h^{\star}_{k}(w_{1}):=\begin{cases}h^{\star}(w_{1})&{\rm for~}w_{1}\in[\frac{1}{k},l],\\
k\left(h^{\star}(\frac{1}{k})-{h^{\star}}(0)\right)w_{1}+{h^{\star}}(0)&{\rm for~}w_{1}\in[0,\frac{1}{k}),\end{cases}

(13.11)

where we recall that
h^{\star}(0)=1
(see Theorem
12.6
),
and we set
h^{\star}_{k}(w_{1}):=h^{\star}_{k}(2l-w_{1})
for
w_{1}\in[l,2l]
(see Fig.
21
, right). Notice that
{h^{\star}_{k}}(0)=1
,

h^{\star}_{k}\in{\rm Lip}([0,2l])
and
the convexity of
h^{\star}
implies that also
h^{\star}_{k}
is convex,

h^{\star}_{k}\geq h^{\star}
, and therefore by Lemma
13.1
(i) we see that

(h^{\star}_{k},\psi^{\star}_{k})\in X_{2l}^{{\rm conv}}
,
where

\psi^{\star}_{k}
is the approximation of
\psi^{\star}
in Lemma

13.1
(with
k=m
), see formula
(
13.2
).
Again by Lemma

13.1
,

{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h^{\star}_{k},\psi^{\star}_{k})={\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star}_{k})+\int_{0}^{2l}\left(h^{\star}_{k}(w_{1})-h^{\star}(w_{1})\right)~dw_{1}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}_{2l}(h^{\star},\psi^{\star})
as
k\rightarrow+\infty
.

We start with the construction of
u_{k}
on
C_{k}^{+}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}
. Set

\displaystyle\tau_{k}:[{r_{k}},l]\to[0,l],

\displaystyle\tau_{k}(r):=\frac{l}{l-{r_{k}}}(r-{r_{k}}),

(13.12)

\displaystyle s_{k}:[{r_{k}},l]\times[0,\theta_{k}]\to[-1,1],

\displaystyle s_{k}(r,\theta):=\frac{1+{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r))}{\theta_{k}}\theta-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r)).

(13.13)

Note that
s_{k}(r,\cdot):[0,\theta_{k}]\to[-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r)),1]
is a
bijective increasing function, for any
r\in[r_{k},l]
.
Thus

\displaystyle s_{k}(r,0)=-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r))\quad{\rm for~any}~r\in[r_{k},l],\text{ in particular }s_{k}({r_{k}},0)=-1,

(13.14)

\displaystyle s_{k}(r,\theta_{k})=1,\qquad r\in[{r_{k}},l],

(13.15)

\displaystyle s_{k}(r_{k},\theta)=\frac{2\theta}{\theta_{k}}-1,\qquad\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}].

(13.16)

We have, for all
r\in[{r_{k}},l]
and
\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}]
,

\displaystyle\tau_{k}^{\prime}(r)=\frac{l}{l-{r_{k}}},

(13.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}s_{k}(r,\theta)=\frac{1+{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r))}{\theta_{k}},

(13.18)

and, for almost every
r\in[{r_{k}},l]
and
all
\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}]
,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}s_{k}(r,\theta)=\left(\frac{\theta}{\theta_{k}}-1\right)\tau_{k}^{\prime}(r){h^{\star}_{k}}^{\prime}(\tau_{k}(r))=\frac{l}{l-{r_{k}}}\left(\frac{\theta}{\theta_{k}}-1\right){h^{\star}_{k}}^{\prime}(\tau_{k}(r)).

(13.19)

Moreover we define

R_{k}:[0,l]\to[{r_{k}},l],\qquad R_{k}({w_{1}}):=\frac{l-{r_{k}}}{l}{w_{1}}+{r_{k}}~

(13.20)

to be the inverse of
\tau_{k}
and, recalling that

\overline{R}_{l}=[0,l]\times[-1,1]
,

\Theta_{k}:SG_{{h^{\star}_{k}}}\cap\overline{R}_{l}\to[0,\theta_{k}],\qquad\Theta_{k}({w_{1}},{w_{2}}):=\frac{\theta_{k}}{1+{h^{\star}_{k}}({w_{1}})}({h^{\star}_{k}}({w_{1}})-{w_{2}}).

(13.21)

Notice that

\Theta_{k}({w_{1}},\cdot):[-1,{h^{\star}_{k}}({w_{1}})]\to[0,\theta_{k}]
is a linearly decreasing bijective function.

The map

\mathcal{T}_{k}:C_{k}^{+}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}\to SG_{{h^{\star}_{k}}}\cap\overline{R}_{l},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{k}(r,\theta):=(\tau_{k}(r),-s_{k}(r,\theta)),

(13.22)

is invertible, and its inverse is the map

\mathcal{T}_{k}^{-1}:SG_{{h^{\star}_{k}}}\cap\overline{R}_{l}\to C_{k}^{+}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{k}^{-1}({w_{1}},{w_{2}}):=(R_{k}({w_{1}}),\Theta_{k}({w_{1}},{w_{2}})).

(13.23)

The modulus of the determinant of the Jacobian of
\mathcal{T}_{k}^{-1}
is given by

|J_{\mathcal{T}_{k}^{-1}}|=\left(\frac{l-{r_{k}}}{l}\right)\frac{\theta_{k}}{1+{h^{\star}_{k}}({w_{1}})}.

(13.24)

We set

{u_{k}}(r,\theta):=\Big(s_{k}(r,\theta),\psi^{\star}_{k}\big(\mathcal{T}_{k}(r,\theta)\big)\Big)=\Big(u_{k1}(r,\theta),u_{k2}(r,\theta)\Big),\qquad r\in[r_{k},l],\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}].

(13.25)

Observe that, using the definition of
\psi_{k}^{\star}
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u_{k}}\in{\rm Lip}(C_{k}^{+}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}},\mathbb{R}^{2}),

(13.26)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u_{k}}(r,\theta_{k})=(s_{k}(r,\theta_{k}),\psi_{k}^{\star}(\mathcal{T}_{k}(r,\theta_{k})))=(1,0),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u_{k}}(r,0)=(-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r)),\psi^{\star}_{k}(\tau_{k}(r),{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r))))=(-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r)),0),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u_{k}}({r_{k}},\theta)=(s_{k}({r_{k}},\theta),\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,-s_{k}({r_{k}},\theta)))=(s_{k}({r_{k}},\theta),\varphi_{k}(0,-s_{k}({r_{k}},\theta))),

for
r\in[r_{k},l]
and
\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}]
,
as it follows from
(
13.12
),
(
13.14
), (
13.15
), and (
12.15
),
where
\varphi_{k}
is defined in (
13.1
) (with
k=m
).

Eventually we define
u_{k}
on
C_{k}^{-}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}
as

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(r,\theta):=(u_{k1}(r,2\pi-\theta),-u_{k2}(r,2\pi-\theta)),\qquad r\in[r_{k},l],\theta\in[2\pi-\theta_{k},2\pi).

(13.27)

It turns out

\displaystyle{u_{k}}\in{\rm Lip}(C_{k}^{-}\setminus{{\rm B}_{r_{k}}},\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(r,2\pi-\theta_{k})=(1,0),

\displaystyle{u_{k}}(r,2\pi)=(-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r)),-\psi^{\star}_{k}(\tau_{k}(r),{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r))))=(-{h^{\star}_{k}}(\tau_{k}(r)),0),

\displaystyle{u_{k}}({r_{k}},\theta)=(s_{k}({r_{k}},2\pi-\theta),-\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,-s_{k}({r_{k}},2\pi-\theta))),

for
r\in[r_{k},l]
,
\theta\in[2\pi-\theta_{k},2\pi)
.

The area of the graph of
u_{k}
on
C_{k}\setminus B_{r_{k}/2}

will be computed in step 7.

Step 3.

Definition of
{u_{k}}
on
{{C}_{k}}\cap(\overline{B}_{r_{k}}\setminus{\rm B}_{r_{k}/2})

and its area contribution.

Let
G_{\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
(resp.

G_{\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
)
denote the graph of
\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)
(resp.
of
\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)
)
on
[-1,1]
.
We
introduce the retraction map

\Upsilon:(\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty))\setminus O\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{{\rm target}}\rightarrow G_{\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{{\rm target}}
,
O=(0,0)
, defined by

\displaystyle\Upsilon(p)=q:=G_{\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)}\cap\ell_{Op}\qquad\forall p\in(\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty))\setminus O,

where
\ell_{Op}
is the line passing through

O

and
p
. Then
\Upsilon
is well-defined and it is
Lipschitz continuous in a neighbourhood of
G_{\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)}
in
\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty)
.
We also define

\Upsilon_{k}:G_{\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)}\rightarrow G_{\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)}

as the
restriction of
\Upsilon
to
G_{\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)}
;
see Fig.
22
.
As a consequence, since for
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough
G_{\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,\cdot)}

is contained in a neighbourhood of
G_{\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)}
,
we have that
\Upsilon_{k}
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
independent of
k
.
Notice also that
\Upsilon_{k}((-1,0))=(-1,0)
and
\Upsilon_{k}((1,0))=(1,0)
.

We define
u_{k}
on
C_{k}^{+}\cap(\overline{\rm B}_{r_{k}}\setminus{\rm B}_{r_{k}/2})
setting,
for
r\in[\frac{r_{k}}{2},r_{k}]
and
\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}]
,

\displaystyle u_{k}(r,\theta):=\Big(2-\frac{2r}{r_{k}}\Big)\Upsilon_{k}\big(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\big)+\Big(\frac{2r}{r_{k}}-1\Big)\big(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\big).

We have

u_{k}(r_{k},\theta)=(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))),

so that
u_{k}
glues, on
C_{k}^{+}\cap\partial{\rm B}_{r_{k}}
,
with the values obtained in step 2 (last formula in (
13.26
)), and

u_{k}(r,\theta_{k})=(1,0),\qquad u_{k}(r,0)=(-1,0).

This formula shows that
u_{k}
also glues,
on
C_{k}^{+}\cap\{(r,\theta):r\in[r_{k}/2,r_{k}],\theta\in\{0,\theta_{k}\}\}
,
with the values obtained in step 2 (second and third formula in (
13.26
)). Moreover

u_{k}(r_{k}/2,\theta)=\Upsilon_{k}\big(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\big),\qquad\theta\in[0,\theta_{k}].

(13.28)

In addition, the derivatives of
u_{k}
satisfy,
for
r\in(\frac{r_{k}}{2},r_{k})
and
\theta\in(0,\theta_{k})
, using (
13.16
),

\displaystyle\partial_{r}u_{k}(r,\theta)=-\frac{2}{r_{k}}\Upsilon_{k}\big(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\big)+\frac{2}{r_{k}}\big(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\big),

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}u_{k}(r,\theta)=\Big(2-\frac{2r}{r_{k}}\Big)\nabla\Upsilon_{k}\big(s_{k}(r_{k},\theta),\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\big)\cdot\Big(\frac{2}{\theta_{k}},-\frac{2}{\theta_{k}}\partial_{w_{2}}\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\Big(\frac{2r}{r_{k}}-1\Big)\Big(\frac{2}{\theta_{k}},-\frac{2}{\theta_{k}}\partial_{w_{2}}\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))\Big),

so that

\displaystyle|\partial_{r}u_{k}(r,\theta)|\leq\frac{4}{r_{k}},

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}u_{k}(r,\theta)|\leq\frac{2(\widehat{C}+1)}{\theta_{k}}(|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,-s_{k}(r_{k},\theta))|+1),

where
\widehat{C}
is a positive constant independent of
k
, which bounds the gradient of
\Upsilon_{k}
.
Since
\psi_{k}^{\star}

is Lipschitz, we deduce that
u_{k}
is Lipschitz
continuous
76
76

76

The Lipschitz constant of
u_{k}
on this set turns out to be unbounded with respect to
k
.

on
C_{k}^{+}\cap({{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}\setminus{\rm B}_{r_{k}/2})
.

Furthermore the image of
(\frac{r_{k}}{2},r_{k})\times(0,\theta_{k})
through the map
(r,\theta)\mapsto u_{k}(r,\theta)

is the
region enclosed by
G_{\psi^{\star}_{k}}
and
G_{\psi^{\star}}
(with multiplicity
1
).
The area of this region is infinitesimal as
k\rightarrow+\infty
,
so that, by the area formula,

\int_{r_{k}/2}^{r_{k}}\int_{0}^{\theta_{k}}r|Ju_{k}(r,\theta)|d\theta dr=o(1)\qquad{\rm as~}k\rightarrow+\infty.

Hence, using the fact that the gradient in polar
coordinates is
(\partial_{r},\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta})
,
we eventually estimate (see also (
2.3
))

\displaystyle\int_{r_{k}/2}^{r_{k}}\int_{0}^{\theta_{k}}r|\mathcal{M}(\nabla u_{k})|d\theta dr

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r_{k}/2}^{r_{k}}\int_{0}^{\theta_{k}}\Big(r+\frac{4r}{r_{k}}+\frac{C}{\theta_{k}}|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,1-\frac{2\theta}{\theta_{k}})|+\frac{C}{\theta_{k}}\Big)~d\theta dr+o(1),

\displaystyle=o(1)+C\frac{r_{k}}{2\theta_{k}}\int_{0}^{\theta_{k}}|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi_{k}^{\star}(0,1-\frac{2\theta}{\theta_{k}})|d\theta=o(1)

(13.29)

as
k\rightarrow+\infty
.
In the last equality we use that
|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)|\leq|\partial_{w_{2}}\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)|
, which is integrable via the change of variables
w_{2}=1-\frac{2\theta}{\theta_{k}}
(it also makes
\theta_{k}
disappear at the denominator in front of the integral
in (
13
)).

This proves that
the contribution of area of the graph of

u_{k}
over
C_{k}^{+}\cap({{\rm B}_{r_{k}}}\setminus{\rm B}_{r_{k}/2})
is infinitesimal as
k\rightarrow+\infty
.

Figure 22:
the graphs of the functions
\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)
and
\psi^{\star}(0,\cdot)
; these contain arcs of circle centered at
(0,0)
and
(0,-\frac{2}{k})
respectively. The map
\Upsilon_{k}
is emphasized. This turns out to be the restriction of
x\mapsto\frac{x}{|x|}
on
\psi^{\star}_{k}(0,\cdot)
.

Eventually, for
r\in[r_{k}/2,r_{k}]
,
\theta\in[2\pi-\theta_{k},2\pi)
, we set

\displaystyle u_{k}(r,\theta):=(u_{k1}(r,2\pi-\theta),-u_{k2}(r,2\pi-\theta)).

(13.30)

Observe that, thanks to (
13.27
),
u_{k}
is continuous on
\partial{\rm B}_{r_{k}}
, and
similar estimates as in (
